### **Project Summary** 

The world's population is aging rapidly, and for many, longer life brings more years in poor health. Urban environments shape how individuals age. However, no study has characterized the full urban exposome in relation to healthy aging. Individual exposures such as greenspace, walkability, fine particulate matter, noise, and heat have been linked to isolated aging outcomes, but these studies typically examine one or two exposures at a time or focus on single domains of aging. No study has integrated built, social, and ambient exposures simultaneously across the biological, physical, cognitive, psychological, and social dimensions of healthy aging. Because policies operate within a complex urban context where no single factor acts in isolation, understanding the joint contributions of urban exposures is essential for translating epidemiological evidence into actionable policy. 

Our overall goal is to characterize the independent and joint associations between the urban exposome and healthy aging, and to project the potential impact of feasible urban interventions on aging outcomes. We pursue three specific aims: 

**Aim 1:  Assess associations between the Urban Exposome and physiological and metabolic aging indicators.** Using ~20,000 patients (~160,000 person-years) from Mount Sinai Health System’s Electronic Health Records, we will derive Phenotypic Age Acceleration and Veterans Affairs Frailty Index. We will estimate the joint mixture association and the relative contribution of each exposure to both outcomes using quantile G- computation. 

**Aim 2: Assess associations between the Urban Exposome and physical capability, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing aging metrics.** Using 900 Chicago participants with up to 15 years of follow-up from the Health Literacy and Cognitive Function Among Older Adults cohort, we will simultaneously estimate associations between the urban exposome and both the baseline level and rate of change of physical function, cognition, depression, and social support. We will apply Bayesian Varying Coefficient Kernel Machine Regression. 

**Aim 3: Explore the projected population health impact of feasible urban environmental interventions on healthy aging.** Using machine-learning predictions, we will project healthy aging outcomes under scenarios in which specific urban exposures are modified to levels already observed in comparable neighborhoods. 

This proposal is innovative in being the first to characterize the full urban exposome in relation to extensive healthy aging metrics. Its significance lies in generating actionable evidence that urban decision-makers can use directly.

### **PROJECT NARRATIVE** 

Where someone lives shapes how they age and, for the majority of older adults, that means a city. In this proposal, we investigate how built, social, and ambient features of the urban environment jointly shape healthy aging across biological, physical, cognitive, psychological, and social domains. Findings will give urban planners, public health agencies, and policymakers the evidence they need to design cities that support healthy aging.

### **FACILITIES AND RESOURCES** 

### **THE ICAHN SCHOOL OF MEDICINE AT MOUNT SINAI (ISMMS)** 

ISMMS was founded in 1967. It is a hospital-based school of medicine, and it grew from and remains closely joined to the 1,171-bed Mount Sinai Hospital, which was established in New York City in 1852. The ISMMS includes 34 academic departments and 23 multidisciplinary institutes spanning the breadth of medical research and basic sciences with an annual budget of $1.9 billion. The ISMMS, the Mount Sinai Hospital and the Mount Sinai Graduate School of Biological Sciences form the Mount Sinai Medical Center, located in northern Manhattan. It straddles the boundary between Manhattan’s Upper East Side, one of the wealthiest neighborhoods in America, and East Harlem, a vibrant urban community where 39% of residents live in poverty, and rates of infant mortality, childhood asthma, obesity, diabetes and hypertension are among the highest in the United States. Mount Sinai is a major provider of medical care to both of these communities. 

### **DEPARTMENTS** 

**<u>Department of Environmental Medicine</u>** is a nationally and internationally recognized center of excellence in preventive medicine, occupational and environmental medicine, community health and environmental pediatrics. The National Institute for Occupational Safety & Health (NIOSH) has designated the Department the major provider of clinical care and epidemiological follow-up to the survivors of the attacks on the World Trade Center of September 11, 2001, and the Department is currently providing diagnostic and treatment services to more than 20,000 9/11 responders. Today the Department of Environmental Medicine and Public Health consists of 44 full-time faculty, 164 adjunct and voluntary faculty, and 266 staff. It has an annual budget of $60.7 million. It is housed principally in Mount Sinai’s Center for Advanced Medicine located at 17 East 102<sup>nd</sup> Street in Manhattan and occupies 20,053 square feet, including lecture and seminar rooms, offices, and laboratories. The Department is highly interdisciplinary and undertakes research in preventive medicine, public health, epidemiology, environmental and occupational medicine and the behavioral sciences. It educates medical students, MPH students, residents and fellows. It provides clinical services to workers exposed to occupational hazards, to workers and volunteers from the World Trade Center disaster, and to children exposed to toxic chemicals in the environment. It also provides a wide array of preventive services in East Harlem and in the other communities that Mount Sinai serves. The Department houses seven divisions: Biostatistics, Environmental Epidemiology, Environmental Health, General Preventive Medicine, International Health, Occupational Medicine, as well as Social Work and Behavioral Sciences. 

The PI will have the support of Dr. Vishal Midya (Co-I), a statistician from the **Division of Biostatistics** in the Department of Environmental Medicine. The Division of Biostatistics includes numerous statisticians receiving support to provide consultation and support for environmental health research by the Biostatistics and Bioinformatics Facility Core of the **_Mount Sinai Transdisciplinary Center on Health Effects of Early Environmental Exposures_** _NIEHS P30 Center_ . This unit has served as a data-coordinating center in multicenter investigations, and it has experience in establishing databases, generating quarterly reports from ongoing studies, and providing final analyses of data at the conclusion of longitudinal studies. Dr. Chris Gennings, Director of the Division of Biostatistics **,** is a leading mixtures biostatistician who has pioneered the use of the weighted quantile sum (WQS) regression technique. She is the leader of the Biostatistics/Bioinformatics Facility Core and has led many studies on environmental chemical mixtures and health outcomes. Faculty in the Division are course directors for the various courses in biostatistics that are part of the core curriculum in the Graduate School of Biomedical Sciences and core curriculum in the Master of Public Health program. 

**<u>Windreich Department of Artificial Intelligence and Human Health</u>** has been given a mission to transform the care of the patient across the Mount Sinai Health System. In this state-of-the-art facility, and in collaboration with other scientific computing and data partners, Icahn Mount Sinai now can analyze tremendous quantities of medical data. With this new ability, building an **_“intelligent fabric”_** that will provide a framework for all biomedicine and health care within the Mount Sinai Health System. The department plans to leverage existing productivity and support tools for diagnosis, treatment, and workflow optimization. Simultaneously, it seeks to connect these tools to significantly enhance patient outcomes. The following resources are available under the department: 1. _Artificial Intelligence and Emerging Technologies in Medicine_ 2. _Minerva High Performance Computing (HPC)_ : Facilitates complex simulations and data analyses at high speeds. 3. _The Charles Bronfman Institute for Personalized Medicine:_ Focuses on personalized healthcare solutions using genetic profiles to

**<u>The Department of Public Health</u>** is headquartered at 1399 Park Avenue at the ISMMS and is chaired by Dr. Rosalind Wright (chair) and Dr. Damaskini Valvi (Vice-chair). The Department of Public Health was founded in 2024 with the goal to modernize and advance innovations in public health research and education across ISMMS and oversees the Public Health and Health Administration master-level and PhD programs at ISMMS. The 1399 Park Avenue location includes sufficient space for support staff and office equipment including scanners, fax, and photocopy machines and office space for faculty, research field staff, programmers/data managers, and administrative staff. 

**<u>Department of Medicine:</u>** The Department of Medicine has a strong record of clinical, epidemiology, and outcomes research. The Department has approximately 135 funded Principal Investigators engaged in a vast array of basic and clinical research and is ranked in the top 15 of funding by the NIH, annually receiving nearly $100 million in funding. The faculty, in collaboration with other investigators, is involved in research activities spanning the hospital, ambulatory care, and home care settings. The projects include research on cancer epidemiology, chronic disease self-management, and comparative effectiveness research. The Department holds regular research meetings to discuss ongoing studies and review potential research opportunities. As part of this study, we will use these meetings to review and receive feedback from our colleagues regarding study results, data interpretation, manuscript preparation, and potential venues for publication. The Department holds regular research meetings to discuss ongoing studies and review potential research opportunities. As part of this study, we will use these meetings to review our project and receive feedback from our colleagues regarding study results, data interpretation, manuscript preparation, and potential venues for publication. 

<u>Division of General Internal Medicine: The Division of General Internal Medicine is the largest division in the</u> Department of Medicine and is the largest primary care practice in the Mount Sinai Healthcare System. The Division has a well-established section of research, with 10 full time research faculty and a research staff of >80, including project managers, bilingual research coordinators, and data analysts. The DGIM also leads The Mount Sinai T32 Program in Cancer Prevention and Control, a training program funded by the National Cancer Institute and a T32 Program in Research in Aging and Dementia (Dr. Federman, Co-I). 

**<u>Brookdale Department of Geriatrics and Palliative Medicine:</u>** Consistently ranked as one of the top three academic programs in the nation, the Department provides expert clinical care for the most frail and complex patients; conducts research in palliative care and aging with an emphasis on medical decision-making, quality, differences in access to palliative care, healthcare delivery models, symptomatology and health services research; and teaches geriatrics and palliative medicine to medical students, house staff, attending physicians and other health professionals. Currently, the department has 52 full-time faculty members at the main Mount Sinai campus and 20 additional faculty members at 4 other Mount Sinai hospitals. The department sponsors active clinical research programs in geriatrics and in palliative care with projects funded by the NIH, HRSA, CMMI, the VA, and over 15 philanthropic foundations. In 2009 (renewed in 2014 and 2020), the department was awarded a Claude D. Pepper Older Americans Independence Center (OAIC) from NIA. This $5.8 million, 5-year NIA grant provides methodologic, measurement, career development, and pilot project support for investigators studying the impact of symptoms and care delivery models on outcomes in older adults with serious illness; it unites investigators from six departments at Mount Sinai, the James J. Peters Veterans Administration Medical Center, the Hebrew Home for the Aged in Riverdale, and the New Jewish Home 

<u>The Lilian and Benjamin Hertzberg Palliative Care Institute, located within the Brookdale Department of</u> Geriatrics and Palliative Medicine, is a hospital-based palliative care program encompassing a broad array of clinical, educational and research activities aimed at improving the care of persons with serious illnesses. 

<u>The Patty and Jay Baker National Palliative Care Research Center, established in 2014 by a $10 million gift, is</u> located within the Department of Geriatrics and Palliative Medicine. The Center supports public policy, education, training, national outreach, and research aimed at improving access to quality palliative care for all seriously ill Americans and their families by uniting the National Palliative Care Research Center (NPCRC) and the Center to Advance Palliative Care (CAPC) under one umbrella. The Center has forged strong working relationships with: The Joint Commission, American Cancer Society/ACS-CAN, Institute for Healthcare Improvement (IHI), The

**<u>Department of Population Health Science and Policy</u>** is one of the academic departments of the ISMMS specializing in quality of care, health services, clinical outcomes, and health policy research. The Department's activities include a broad variety of research and quality improvement projects funded by federal, state, foundation, and hospital sources. Its team of multidisciplinary faculty, data managers, analysts, and experts in clinical data collection and health outcomes research are investigating a variety of topics including: breast cancer, diabetes prevention, and cardiovascular disease. 

### **RESEARCH SUPPORT** 

**<u>The GREENER platform:</u>** Led by Dr. Kloog (Co-I), through the Mount Sinai CTSA, the GREENER platform (Geospatial Reproducible Environmental Exposure National Exposome Resource) serves as a highperformance geospatial data infrastructure designed to revolutionize environmental health research. This unique repository facilitates the efficient storage, visualization, and sharing of large-scale environmental datasets, including ambient and urban environmental exposures. By providing tools for custom area selection and flexible temporal analysis, GREENER enables researchers to precisely define and query environmental exposures. Ultimately, the platform bridges the gap between complex geospatial big data and actionable precision environmental health research insights, supporting the broader mission of the CTSA to promote translational research. 

**<u>Mount Sinai Data Warehouse (MSDW) Ecosystem:</u>** <u><mark>Electronic Health Record Data is collected by MSDW.</mark></u> <mark>MSDW ecosystem uses the Observational Medical Outcomes Partnership (OMOP) Common Data Model to facilitate optimal data sharing and interoperability both internally (at Mount Sinai) and externally with research partners. The clinical data are extracted from Mount Sinai’s Epic Caboodle database and other ancillary systems, transformed to the OMOP Common Data Model format, and loaded to the MSDW database. The data is curated in a way that ensures consistency across various healthcare settings. Extraction of medical data is done through a singular electronic health records system i.e., EPIC. Data is assembled on structured clinical concepts such as SNOMED - systematically organized computer-processable collection of medical terms</mark> . 

**<u>Office of Research Services:</u>** Developed in response to the needs of the Mount Sinai Health System’s (MSHS) expansion and the parallel evolving complexity of research regulations, policies and processes, the Office of Research Services (ORS) was established to facilitate the conduct of translational and clinical research through a centralized infrastructure supported by Condu _ITS’_ Administrative Core and the Office of the Dean of Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai.  The ORS provides oversight of program organization, evaluation, continuous improvement, quality, efficiency, safety and communications allowing ConduITS to continually identify barriers, gaps and unmet needs of the MSHS research community and to implement integrated solutions that impact the scope of Mount Sinai’s learning healthcare system. 

Given the increasing need for innovative informatics solutions to managing the workflow for translational sciences across the MSHS, the ORS has developed e-tools to assist researchers find answer to questions and provide navigation of the MSHS research infrastructure. These platforms also collect metrics about utilization, issues and customer satisfaction to assist in the continuous improvement of the services provided by the ORS. In 2018, the ORS launched the health system’s global research help desk, Research 411, which served approximately 1,000 individuals. The ConduITS Research Roadmap (RRM) was implemented in Q2 2019. The RRM is a web-based, interactive, real-time tool that provides MSHS researchers with navigation of the health system’s research operations, workflows, resources and imbeds real-time and evolving regulatory guidance and best practices to provide the highest quality conduct of research at the MSHS. The RRM also provides a centralized, dedicated environment for ConduITS to communicate advances to the network of CTSA hubs across the nation. The ORS also led the implementation of the institution’s system-wide clinical trials management system which will further streamline research study conduct, oversight and efficiency locally and remotely across an ever-expanding health system and collaborating institutions. 

**<u>Research Roadmap (RRM):</u>** The Research Roadmap is a robust and comprehensive web-based, centralized tool being developed by the ORS. The RRM, visually designed to function like an interactive subway map, was

developed to provide system-wide discovery of resoures and navigation of the many “pathways” of the MSHS research infrastructure and  provides a “line” to the CTSA national network of resources. It is an interactive, dynamic, easy to use tool that provides the most current, streamlined, and efficient access to administrative processes, external regulatory requirements, internal policies, most current news, education and training opportunities toward the conduct of the highest quality research. The RRM has increased efficiency by providing information for parallel processing, monitoring of timelines and review processes. Locally designed and built, the RRM is complementary to the Research411 portal as a tangible, immediately accessible and continuously available web-based tool and platform for the dissemination of the “tips” and “best practices” learned through the Research 411 portal. The RRM increases productivity time by reducing the discovery time for research teams to understand research methods and processes. Controlled by the ORS, the team can real-time post the newest modifications and news for the system.  Dissemination of RRM improvements is provided by the ORS centrally via the listserv and availability of local hub innovations and tools is provided through a dedicated “line” for the CTSA national network of hubs and the CTSA program.  The location of solutions that are developed for gaps and bottlenecks in research systems will be more efficiently pinpointed and communicated on the RRM. 

### **SCIENTIFIC COMPUTING** 

**<u>Minerva High Performance Computer Cluster:</u>** The primary asset for Scientific Computing is the supercomputer, Minerva. The HPC resource, Minerva, was created in 2012 and has been upgraded several times, most recently in Nov. 2024, utilizes 24,912 Intel Platinum in different generations including 8568Y+ 2.3GHz, 8358 2.6 GHz, and 8268 2.9 GHz compute cores (96 cores or 64 cores or 48 cores per node with two sockets in each node) with 1.5TB of memory per node, 196 H100 GPUs, 32 L40S, 40 A100 GPUs, 48 V100 GPUs, 440 terabytes of total memory, 32 petabytes of spinning storage accessed via IBM’s Spectrum Scale/General Parallel File System (GPFS) for a total of > 2 petaflops of CPU compute power and ~ 8 petaflops for GPU compute power. 

(see Equipment section). 

**<u>Computing support:</u>** The Mount Sinai Levy Library has a Computer Help Desk that provides extensive technical support for email, desktops and network file storage requests and problems. In addition, they distribute a number of site-licensed software products for both business productivity and statistical analysis. Some of the statistical packages that they offer are SAS, and ArcGIS. Mount Sinai provides network connectivity to the Internet via both wired and wireless networks. 

**<u>Storage options:</u>** All data are stored on the institution-owned storage servers, with institution-wide access and storage policy governing security and integrity of data. The institutional Information Technology (IT) group provides access for all researchers to a System Area Network (SAN) with storage capacity of over 50 terabytes and redundant units for data synchronization. The servers are maintained in discrete secure locations in a redundant configuration that ensures continuity of service in the event of a flood, fire, hardware failure, or any other type of disaster. Physical access to server rooms and IT/Telecom closets is protected by multiple security layers and limited to authorized personnel. A user’s logical access to data is strictly controlled by the Microsoft Active Directory (AD) services and the institution’s IT security policy, which among other things, requires each user to have a unique ID and a complex password with a 90-day lifespan. Each person’s access to data is assigned on per individual or group basis, and only at the request of the data owner, or an authority designated by data owner. The institution’s current back up policy requires 8 weeks (56 days) of backup data to be stored on our SAN. This data is backed up first to a magnetic library (disk-2-disk copies) where it is kept for 14 days and is then subsequently copied to a tape library (magnetic tapes) which are kept for the duration of the following 6 weeks at which time each tape set is purged and recycled for reuse. While in storage, the tape sets are transferred on a weekly basis to a different building on campus where they are kept until the end of the 8-week cycle. Institution security policy prohibits storage of data on local user workstations, or portable media; remote access to data is limited to VPN connected users with legitimate business need.

### <mark>FACILITIES AND RESOURCES</mark> 

Northwestern University (NU) supports and promotes a vibrant and ever-growing community of clinical and aging research scientists that are passionately committed to improving human health. Some broad highlights of the structure of NU and the substantial resources available to investigators in this community are detailed immediately below, followed on succeeding pages by a more detailed description of the wide range of resources available to the research enterprise at Northwestern, including our clinical partners. 

### <mark>NORTHWESTERN UNIVERSITY</mark> 

NU supports and promotes a vibrant and ever-growing community of clinical and aging research scientists that are passionately committed to improving human health. NU is one of the country's leading private research universities, with an annual budget of $3.3 billion and sponsored research in excess of $1 billion (FY24). Northwestern has a long history of leadership in interdisciplinary research programs and centers. More than 90 school-based and 30 University centers support interdisciplinary research that spans a wide spectrum of areas. In support of its research, educational, and service missions, NU provides an exceptional backbone of information technology and communications resources to fuel continual research innovation and scholarship. The University is 12th among the nation’s private universities and the 8 libraries provide comprehensive resources and research services in every major field totaling more than 6 million volumes and 341 TB of digital content (and counting). The University information technology program supports researchers with the software, hardware, data storage and retrieval facilities to conduct large-scale projects. Northwestern’s strong culture for collaborative research has played an important role in its diverse and innovative research enterprise. We believe that Northwestern is exceptionally well-positioned as a performance site for this study because of its world-class facilities and resources, experienced faculty, evaluation capacities, and strong research administration. 

### **Feinberg School of Medicine** 

Feinberg School of Medicine is located on the Chicago campus. The school is ranked 13th among US medical schools (research) by U.S. News & World Report, and Feinberg research awards increased to $742.2 million in FY 2024, of which $461 million was from the National Institutes of Health. Individual grant awards included 89 individual research fellowships (F awards), 74 career development awards (K awards), and 35 training grants (T awards). As testament to the effective response to the pandemic, more than $34 million in FY 2020 awards were addressing research on COVID-19. Over the last 11 years, Feinberg’s NIH portfolio has grown by 129 percent, from $208 million to $477 million per year. At the same time, the medical school’s overall research portfolio has grown by 124 percent. More than 6,902 clinical trials and research studies were conducted at Feinberg in the 2023-20243 academic year, led by 724 principal investigators and 505 principal investigators on NIH grants. Additionally, a total of 40 patents and six new start-up companies were established within the last fiscal year alone. In FY2024 there were a total of 371,561 participants enrolled in clinical trials. Feinberg faculty on all professional tracks number about 4,830. 

Northwestern University celebrated the opening of the Louis A. Simpson and Kimberly K. Querrey Biomedical Research Center building June 17, 2019. The building doubles vivarium capacity and houses laboratories for both Feinberg and the Stanley Manne Children’s Research Institute, the research arm of the Ann and Robert H. Lurie Children’s Hospital of Chicago. Feinberg has approximately 1,366,000 net square feet of space, of which just over 784,000sf is dedicated to research. Total space includes over 112,000sf in the new Simpson Querrey Biomedical Research Center and over 120,000sf of wet lab space renovated within the last five years as well as 100,000 square feet of newly-acquired dry lab research space over the same time period. Since 2009, Feinberg has invested more than $150,000,000 in renovating more than 400,000 net square feet of owned and leased space. In addition, the affiliated Stanley Manne Children’s Research Institute occupies 93,000sf in the Simpson Querrey Biomedical Research Center. 

### **The Division of General Internal Medicine** 

The Division of General Internal Medicine (GIM) at Northwestern University’s Feinberg School of Medicine is nationally recognized for excellence in health care research and analysis. Faculty members in GIM are currently funded by the National Institutes of Health, the Agency for Health Care Research and Quality, Centers for Disease Control, Patient Centered Outcomes Research Institute, and a number of foundations. Through strong leadership, faculty expertise, and the specialized skills of project managers, the research programs in GIM spearhead a broad and innovative, nationally- 

Page 12 

Facilities & Other Resources 



<!-- page 14 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

recognized research enterprise that focuses on health services research, healthcare policy analysis, health communication, vascular disease, the use of electronic health records, reduced disparity for historically underserved communities, novel physician education methods, health equity, and quality improvement and patient safety. GIM’s primary administrative and research offices comprise approximately 22,000 square feet of space in the Arthur Rubloff Building of Northwestern’s downtown Chicago campus. There are weekly Work in Progress conferences and a weekly health services research seminar series for faculty to present their work. Faculty from across Northwestern present at these conferences, in addition to invited local and national speakers, which creates a collaborative environment in which people work to develop and refine each other’s ideas, as well as offer an opportunity for junior faculty to receive feedback on proposed research projects. 

### **The Institute for Public Health and Medicine (IPHAM)** 

Founded in 2012, the Institute for Public Health and Medicine (IPHAM) is a research institute at Northwestern University that partners closely with academic departments such as Medicine, Preventive Medicine, and Medical Social Sciences to provide a nexus for all public and population health activities at the Feinberg School of Medicine. The goal of the Institute is to accelerate innovation at the interface of medicine and public health and achieve measurable improvements in health for patients and populations. IPHAM's 16 centers provide an organizational structure, with foci spanning the spectrum from communities to the individual, and from health behaviors to genetic determinants of disease. Whereas most medical school departments are organized by specific clinical or methodological disciplines, IPHAM centers are designed to support interdisciplinary education and research activities that focus on pressing theme areas of policy, practice, public and population health, and health equity. IPHAM currently provides a home for 600+ Northwestern faculty members across 51 departments in seven Northwestern schools to engage with community partners and other stakeholders in multidisciplinary research and educational activities. Naturally, this means that most IPHAM members are also NUCATS members and are high users of NUCATS’ resources and services. By working collaboratively with NUCATS, IPHAM centers need not duplicate resources and services necessary for successful clinical and translational science. Instead, IPHAM Centers focus strategically on providing interactive office space, catalytic meetings and presentations, and fiscal and leadership support to ensure the growth and impact of specific research programs focused primarily on the clinical to population end of the translational spectrum. 

The Institute has the following centers: Center for Applied Health Research on Aging, Center for Behavior and Health, Center for Bioethics and Medical Humanities, Buehler Center for Health Policy and Economics,  Center for Community Health, Center for Communication and Health, Center for Education in Health Sciences, Center for Engineering and Health, Center for Epidemiology and Population Health, Center for Food Allergy & Asthma Research, Center for Health Equity Transformation, Center for Health Information Partnerships, Center for Health Services & Outcomes Research, Center for Patient-Centered Outcomes, Center for Primary Care Innovation, and the Center for Translational Metabolism and Health. 

### Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA) 

Directed by Michael Wolf, PhD, MPH, MA, CAHRA serves as a research and education hub for geriatricians, gerontologists and aging health services researchers. This Center unites >70 faculty across clinical disciplines and from cognitive, behavioral, social and public health sciences throughout the university and the Northwestern Medicine health system. CAHRA’s mission is to promote informed decision making and actions leading to optimal health and well-being among individuals and families over the life span. 

Building off of Dr. Wolf’s highly successful Health Literacy and Learning Program (HeLP), which was founded in 2004 to create a network of multidisciplinary faculty that could partner together to generate applied, innovative research that would help transform the delivery of healthcare for vulnerable patients (older adults are a specific target), CAHRA was launched in 2019 through an institution-created endowment and is now formally linked to IPHAM and the Department of Medicine. The Center is physically and strategically co-located on the same floor of the Arthur Rubloff Building as the Division of GIM, and encompasses approximately 2,500 square feet of office space, in additional to dedicated research interview space. 

The establishment of CAHRA demonstrates Northwestern’s commitment to prioritizing aging research and to pursuing a leadership position in the field. The Center’s six research programs are a reflection of the wide-ranging research portfolio of the Center’s core faculty members, many of whom are included in this proposal (Dr. Wolf and Ms. Yoshino Benavente): 1) Health Literacy & Learning, 2) Cognitive Aging, 3) Psychosocial Support, 4) Life Course Health, 5) Treatment Adherence, and 6) Measurement & Analysis. Together, CAHRA leads a robust research agenda to explore and understand the many factors that affect a person’s ability to manage health throughout their life and use that knowledge to develop and

### **Northwestern Pepper Center** 

<mark>In 2020, the National Institute on Aging (NIA) awarded Northwestern University a grant to establish the Northwestern Older Americans Independence Center (OAIC), or ‘Pepper Center’, joining a network of 15 Centers across the country to investigate ways to maintain or restore independence in older adults. Co-led by Drs. Michael Wolf and Jeffrey Linder, the Pepper Center serves as one of two hubs for geriatrics research expertise and information dissemination in the Midwest. The Pepper Center is</mark> situated within the Feinberg School of Medicine at Northwestern University and is administratively housed in The Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA), a member center of the Institute for Public Health and Medicine (IPHAM). It closely partners with the Department of Medicine, in the Division of General Internal Medicine & Geriatrics, and the Clinical and Translational Sciences Institute (NUCATS) and leverages many of their existing and available resources. The Northwestern Pepper Center is comprised of faculty members throughout multiple departments and centers (Medical Social Sciences, Preventive Medicine, Mesulam Center for Cognitive Neurology and Alzheimer’s Disease) within the University, many of whom are leaders of their respective departments. The Pepper Center extends beyond Northwestern University, with strong academic partnerships with the University of Illinois at Chicago and Rush University, local and state governments, industry, Northwestern-affiliated clinical partners, and community clinical partners.

The **IDC** ensures bi-directional information flows that deliver usable research findings and products to diverse stakeholders and networks. This Core leverages existing technologies and other resources at Northwestern or through affiliates to meet information needs of local, regional and national primary care networks and other partners to rapidly translate clinical research findings, share ‘best practices’ and support their implementation. 

#### RESEARCH EDUCATION CORE (REC) 

The **REC** incorporates a comprehensive approach to help junior faculty “Pepper Scholars” effectively match the resources to their career development needs and succeed in aging-related research to facilitate care to older adults MCC. 

<mark>The</mark> **<mark>Pepper Scholars Program</mark>** <mark>supports the educational activities and tailored mentorship programs that enhance workforce training to meet the nation's need to serve older Americans with MCC.</mark> 

#### PILOT/EXPLORATORY STUDIES CORE (PESC) 

The **PESC** is a primary home to both our NU Pepper Center’s science and scholarly training. We have strategically linked our **PESC** with our **REC** , with all pilot projects awarded to junior faculty ‘Pepper Scholars’. Pilot projects are competitively reviewed and selected with awards equivalent to an NIH R03 grant ($100,000 direct costs over 2 years) - sufficient to gather preliminary data that can support a K or R mechanism federal grant submission. Projects receive additional ‘in-kind’ design, <u>measurement and analytic support from our three</u> **Resource Cores,** and our IDC will help the ‘Pepper Scholars’ widely share research findings via multiple novel modalities, across large and diverse clinical, research, health policy and lay audiences. 

### Resource Cores 

HEALTHCARE SYSTEM & TECHNOLOGY DESIGN CORE (DESIGN CORE) 

The **Design Core** supports investigators in technology-enabled services research that promote health and well-being among older adults with MCC and facilitates ongoing engagement with primary care. This Core provides expertise to junior investigators, Core and National Pepper Center Network faculty in the design, development, evaluation, and implementation of technology-enabled services for older adults with MCC. 

The Core will be guided by Design Core Director Dr. David Mohr’s Center for Behavioral Intervention Technologies (CBITs) and an evidence-based, Accelerated Create-to-Sustainment (ACTS) model that employs state of the art user-centered design and implementation science to engage all key stakeholders including patients, primary care providers, and administrators in the design and evaluation processes. 

#### PATIENT-REPORTED OUTCOMES MEASUREMENT CORE (MEASUREMENT CORE) 

The **Measurement Core** provides measurement expertise for the entire Northwestern Pepper Center and national Pepper Center network. This Core also serves as a resource to the REC for junior faculty seeking skills in measurement and the deployment of tools in primary care. 

<mark>Northwestern University and the Department of Medical Social Sciences (Chair: Dr. David Cella; Director, Measurement Core) have internationally-recognized and longstanding patient-reported outcomes assessment expertise, including leveraging internet and mobile technology for diverse patient populations. This expertise has been developed over the past 15 years as part of three large-scale NIH initiatives: the Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System (PROMIS), ‘Neuro-QoL’, and the NIH Toolbox.</mark> 

QUANTITATIVE AND QUALITATIVE DATA ANALYTICS CORE (ANALYTICS CORE) 

<mark>The</mark> **<mark>Analytics Core</mark>** <mark>provides expertise on quantitative and qualitative research design and analytic methods to support affiliated faculty, Cores, and pilot projects. The Northwestern Biostatistics Collaboration Center (BCC) led by Analytics Core Director, Dr. Leah Welty, offers an existing infrastructure that is leveraged to support OAIC initiatives. The Analytics Core also supports the rapid growth for qualitative methods that over time may become a formalized institutional aging resource like the BCC.</mark> 

### **Northwestern University Clinical and Translational Sciences Institute (NUCATS)**

<mark>The NUCATS Institute represents a significant collaborative effort involving faculty from across all of Northwestern University, as well as our three top-ten US News ranked clinical affiliates (Northwestern Memorial HealthCare Corporation (NMHC)/Northwestern Memorial Hospital (NMH); Lurie Children’s Hospital; and the Shirley Ryan AbilityLab). NUCATS is currently funded by the National Center for Advancing Translational Sciences (NCATS) of the National Institutes of Health as well as substantial institutional support from Northwestern and our clinical affiliates. The NUCATS Institute is the central hub in Northwestern University’s clinical and translational research enterprise, including six Northwestern schools, all of our clinical affiliates, and community partners and stakeholders. The Institute accelerates translational innovation by providing research teams with consultative resources and expertise and is strategically positioned to remove the roadblocks that slow interdisciplinary clinical and translational research. NUCATS is currently nationally recognized as an innovation leader in several key areas of clinical and translational science including: pioneering novel and scalable approaches in the areas of preclinical and clinical therapeutic and device development; developing health and biomedical informatics systems to promote research; researching networking platforms and training programs to promote development of the next generation of translational scientists and multi-disciplinary team science; and spearheading a regional consortium of the three Chicagoarea Clinical and Translational Science Award (CTSA) programs to promote community engagement activities and practicebased research.</mark> 

<mark>A brief overview of</mark> NUCATS' resources and services is followed by detailed descriptions of the administrative subunits of NUCATS that are most relevant to this proposal. NUCATS administers Clinical Research Units in NU-affiliated adult and children’s hospitals, including dedicated (and flex) inpatient beds, outpatient exam rooms, a preparatory laboratory, and ancillary support offices. In addition, NUCATS offers a wide array of digital and personal research navigation; regulatory support services (including budget and IRB preparation, audit services, clinicaltrials.gov reporting assistance, etc.); clinical study design and coordinator services; a complete array of clinical trials management systems; extensive centralized bioinformatics, biostatistical and epidemiologic support; a large array of community and stakeholder engagement platforms; generous grant-supported and institutionally-funded pilot grant and voucher (micro-grant) funds; and data resources such as the Northwestern Medicine Enterprise Data Warehouse. Above and beyond these resources, NUCATS extensive number of bioinformatics resources for data management, analysis and interpretation supported by knowledgeable and interactive bioinformaticians with a wide range of experience in health and biomedical informatics are integral to the innovative research performed at Northwestern.  NUCATS also houses the Galter Health Sciences Library, allowing integrated access to all of its resources as well. 

### NUCATS Center for Data Science and Informatics (CDSI) 

This Center brings together biomedical informatics, researchers, and clinical informatics leaders to coordinate biomedical informatics across the NU academic medical enterprise. Its mission is to provide coordination for biomedical research activities across Northwestern University and its health care affiliates. To meet this mission, CDSI has culled the necessary expertise and resources to enable and facilitate the application of informatics solutions to clinical and translational research. Grant support is additionally facilitated through NITRO-Competitions, a software platform that hosts internal grant competition among NUCATS partners. NITRO-Competitions supports posting of RFAs, electronic grant submission, and electronic, NIH-style proposal reviews. Additionally, Research Electronic Data Capture (REDCap) is a web-based application to manage basic data capture for research studies. This system will be available for all investigators to customize their data collection. 

### Northwestern Medicine Enterprise Data Warehouse (NMEDW) 

The Northwestern Medicine Enterprise Data Warehouse (NMEDW) is funded and led by leaders from NU and NMHC. It is organizationally housed in CDSI. Created in 2007, the NMEDW serves as the primary vehicle for data integration and transfer for both research and clinical operations. This dual-use model is one of the critical strengths of the NMEDW, and one that enables it to function as a unique bridge, integrating healthcare and research, as well as ensuring support from both the research and clinical partners. NU and NMHC are full NMEDW members. The NMEDW is a jointly-funded initiative, governed by a 9-member steering committee representing both clinical and research users. The NMEDW currently stores over 4.2 trillion observations on 6.4 million patients. Each night, it loads 39.5 billion new data elements from 129 separate sources including electronic health records (EHR), pathology data from the hospital and research laboratories, biomarker data from research databases, and research transactional data from our eIRB and other institutional systems. The NMEDW serves as the primary data integration and transport tool among NUCATS partners. The NMEDW has a mature data steward model, supported by an electronic workflow that enables rapid review of data requests.

### Galter Health Sciences Library 

The Northwestern University Galter Health Sciences Library is uniquely positioned as a Center within the NUCATS Institute, allowing for important synergies between the research, clinical and education activities of Galter and NUCATS. Galter Library is a next-generation fully integrated information and knowledge management hub providing dedicated support for the research, education, and patient care activities at the Feinberg School of Medicine and the McGaw Medical Center. The library’s over 41,000 square feet physical facility provides a comfortable and welcoming environment for collaboration and research with expanded study spaces and 12 conference rooms to facilitate individual and team-based activities. A dedicated portion of the library is also available as a 24-hour facility. A networked conference room with 8 desktop computers provides an inviting space for small-group instruction and online activities. The library’s website provides 24-hour access to a vast collection of online medical resources including over 8,600 e-journals; 3,000 e-books; and 300 databases. Other core library services include a diverse curriculum and research support through the library's liaison program, as well as EndNote and publication support; document delivery to users’ desktops; and wireless access throughout the library. Galter Library carries out its mission through a significant array of forward-looking services and programs: 

- The **integrated clinical informationist program** provides a range of integrated support activities, including rapid support for dissemination of research findings directly to clinical teams on rounds or other clinical forums on focused requested topics using current technology and resources; clinical support services, such as an EHR-based clinical informatics consult service; and collaboration with key constituents on journal clubs, seminars, systematic reviews, and other clinically focused projects. 

- An **enhanced literature searching and systematic review support program** has been highly successful and productive, with several librarians working as active members on systematic review teams. This program continues to grow with ongoing professional development opportunities for all librarians. 

- **Robust support for compliance** of public access policies is offered by Galter. This includes assistance for investigators with NIH Public Access Policy compliance, myNCBI reporting, and assistance with research reporting guidelines by the Northwestern University’s Public Access Compliance Monitor, a Galter librarian. 

- A **steadfast commitment to digital innovation and preservation** , including an enterprise-wide implementation of Symplectic Elements, a bibliographic and grants data aggregator; partnership on the NU enterprise ORCID implementation, and development of the DigitalHub repository to promote open science and discoverability with persistent identifiers, dataset deposit workflows, and machine-readable metadata for a wide variety of research outputs. Galter broadly promotes discoverability through semantic web-based initiatives and related ontology development activities. 

- **The Metrics and Impact Core (MIC)** , a specialized research analytics and visualization core, is focused on the impact of Feinberg research and beyond. The MIC provides impact and justification support for grant submissions and renewals; support for faculty recruitment, tracking, and impact assessment activities; training for enhancing research impact and how to report outcomes to funding agencies; and data analysis and visualizations. The MIC team provides advanced bibliometric and scientometric analysis and offers consultations and workshops on related topics (e.g., enhancing research impact, dissemination strategies, reporting outcomes and impact, visualization strategies). 

- **The Data Lab** is a high-tech data core for training, one-on-one consultations, and building a community of practice around translational informatics, data analysis, visualization, and systems. Galter Library offers centralization of licensed bioinformatics and biostatistics software and information resources with personalized data analysis support, data management consultations, just-in-time online tutorials, and classes. The Data Lab also provides support for issues related to research reproducibility, data analysis, and data visualization. Galter facilitates expert searching and harvesting, NLP analysis, and visualization—encouraging and supporting best practices of transparency, open science, and sound data management. 

- Galter Library supports **community engaged research** through partnerships on health equity initiatives such as The Chicago Cancer Health Equity Collaboration (ChicagoCHEC), funded by the National Cancer Institute and the “Enhancing Clinical Trials Participation though Library Partnerships” project, funded by the National Library of Medicine to support research literacy in diverse populations in coordination with local librarians, community organizations/community health workers, and health care providers. 

- **Strong core library services** , including curriculum and research support through the library's robust liaison program, a publishing and authorship program, a strong metadata and discovery team, and collection services. The library’s

information systems and website provide seamless access to a world-class collection of online medical resources, including electronic journals, books, and hundreds of databases. 

Galter Library additionally offers centralization and provision of licensed bioinformatics and biostatistics software and information resources with personalized data analysis support, data management consultations, and just-in-time online tutorials. The 24-hour space was recently upgraded with a high-level research computing cluster consisting of 6 highpowered research machines loaded with specialized analysis, statistics, and visualization software. Galter Library recently acquired the OpenHelix tutorial suite for the entire Northwestern University research community. The tutorials consist of extensive and effective tutorials and training materials on over 100 of the most powerful and popular genomics resources. 

The Library boasts access to a diverse range of publication databases, facilitating expert searching and enabling harvesting and initial analysis and visualizations of the bibliographic metadata. Tools available at Galter Library include Thompson Reuters Web of Knowledge and Elsevier’s Scopus indices, Journal Citation Reports and Essential Science Indicators by Thomson Reuters and Symplectic Elements, a middleware tool suite that aggregates and deduplicates metadata about research outputs and provides aggregated bibliographic metadata. Galter Library maintains a steadfast commitment to digital innovation and preservation, including traditional efforts to support preservation and provide access to scholarly content through a digital repository, as well as cutting edge efforts to provide better control of the bibliographic metadata of our scholars and to support semantic web-based initiatives and related ontology development activities. 

The DigitalHub Open Repository facilitates the discovery and recognition of research and scholarship at Northwestern Medicine by bringing together, in one place, open access to research, creating a living record of scholarly output. The repository preserves intellectual works created by the Northwestern Medicine scholarly community to enhance the visibility of Northwestern Medicine scholarship and to promote its authors by enabling discovery and accessibility of these works by the international scientific community. DigitalHub has been optimized to create, share and preserve traditional and nontraditional scholarly products, including surveys and other measurement devices, patient brochures, curriculum materials, conference materials, as well as for highly-structured metadata records.  Alternative metrics are also collected and displayed for objects in the Galter repository, allowing social engagement data of all outputs to provide a more robust and timely overview of productivity, community outreach, and the impact of research efforts. 

The Library has promoted several major initiatives to support NU faculty on their research endeavors. A team of librarians are dedicated to assisting investigators with systematic reviews and software for analyses. A Public Access Compliance Monitor assists investigators with NIH Public Access Policy compliance and myNCBI reporting and assistance with research reporting guidelines to ensure compliance of public access policies. Naming of Support extends to issues related to research reproducibility, including consultations and referral of clients to resources on campus that can provide more in-depth support on topics such as experimental design, model organisms, and statistical analysis. The Library also has an integrated clinical informationist program to provide a range of integrated support activities, including: rapid support for dissemination of research findings directly to clinical teams on rounds or other clinical forums on focused requested topics using current technology and resources; clinical support services, such as an EHR-based clinical informatics consult service; and collaboration with key constituents on journal clubs, seminars, systematic reviews, and other clinically focused projects. Finally, the library’s physical facility provides a comfortable and welcoming environment for collaboration and research with expanded study spaces and 12 conference rooms to facilitate individual and team-based activities. The library offers several different environments—from small-group instruction rooms to a large high-tech classroom—and space for collaboration, lectures, and community building initiatives. In 2018, Galter Library launched a major renovation to better provide faculty, researchers, and students with limitless learning opportunities through digital advances and an innovative setting to support students well into the future. The renovation will improve function and enhance the library’s footprint to better support our educational, clinical, and research missions. This begins with a new sky bridge to join the new Louis A. Simpson and Kimberly K. Querrey Biomedical Research Center, promoting interaction and access to library resources such as research software and computing tools. An expanded 24-hour innovation lab work space will serve as a hub for collaboration, investigation, and learning. Flexible, reconfigurable rooms will accommodate large and small group instruction. In select locations, students will be able to display their work individually or in groups on large monitors to catalyze discussion and dialogue. Eleven Problem-Based Learning Rooms will actively support small-group instruction in a technology-rich environment. 

### **Potocsnak Longevity Institute** 

Page 18 

Facilities & Other Resources 



<!-- page 20 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

Founded in 2022, the Longevity Institute works with scientists across disciplines studying populations that seem resistant to some of the negative consequences of aging in an attempt to figure out exactly what makes them different. The Longevity Institute is comprised of the Geroscience Academy as well as 6 complementary and interdisciplinary centers, which include the Potocsnak Center for Aging & HIV, Center for Basic & Translational Biology, Human Longevity Laboratory, Center for Nanoscience & Aging, Center for Population Science & Aging, and the Northwestern Pepper Center. The Institute also partners with the Mesulam Center for Cognitive Neurology and Alzheimer’s Disease as well as the Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA). 

### The Geroscience Academy 

The Academy’s focus is to train and educate future clinicians and scientists with a focus on dynamic and evolving awareness of the science of aging. They will accomplish this by creating formal curriculum informed in the science of aging; developing educational materials and formal courses for GME/CME; hosting community engagement activities including seminars dealing with ethical, economic, political, and societal ramifications of extended human lifespan; sponsoring a global online lecture series; and establishing an annual award for internationally recognized leaders in the field. 

### Potocsnak Center for Aging and HIV 

Through collaborative research activities and videoconferencing across the Northwestern Medicine health system, specialists in the center will engage physicians, investigators, nurses, medical students, physician assistants, nurse practitioner students, residents and fellows across the country and world in active real-time knowledge-sharing in their own communities. The center will promote leading-edge basic, clinical and epidemiological research aimed at: identifying clinical markers and biomarkers of functional aging and associated disease progression among HIV-infected persons, which will become standards for screening and monitoring patient outcomes; generating interventions and clinical strategies to effectively address survival, age-related functional capacity and quality of life; and developing optimal strategies for screening, pre-emptive therapy and treatment among aging HIV-infected persons. 

### Center for Basic & Translational Biology 

The Center provides cognitive and programmatic consolidation of existing programs in the area of aging and longevity. 

### Human Longevity Laboratory 

The Laboratory develops and refines a set of genetic, molecular, physiological and structural measures that reveal biological age and aging-related morbidity in humans. The Chicago Human Longevity Laboratory facilities comprise a 5,000 square foot space in the Arkes Family Pavilion, on Northwestern University’s Chicago Campus. The facilities are outfitted with equipment that allows for comprehensive testing to capture a comprehensive snapshot of patients’ physiological age. 

### Center for Nanoscience & Aging 

Using Northwestern’s expertise in nanoscience, bioengineering and chemistry, this center focuses on the development of new diagnostics and therapeutic devices and agents. 

### Center for Population Science & Aging 

The Center defines the genetic, molecular, proteomic and metabolomic signatures of aging using the power of large human biorepositories and datasets. 

### **The Chicago Area Patient Centered Outcomes Research Network (CAPriCORN)** 

CAPriCORN is one of 13 Clinical Data Research Networks (CDRNs) funded by the Patient Centered Outcomes Research Institute (PCORI), with Northwestern University as one of the founding members. Abel Kho, MD as the PI of CAPriCORN and sits on the coordinating committee and steering committee. He leads the Informatics Working Group and nationally serves as Co-Chair of the Data Standards, Security and Network Infrastructure task force for PCORnet. Dr. Kho's team designed a citywide data mart (HealthLNK database) which forms the basis for CAPriCORN's informatics infrastructure design. For CAPriCORN, there is a current local data mart fully compliant with PCORnet Common Data Model v3.0 and populated with patient data. During 2020, more than 3.1 million patients visited a CAPriCORN-affiliated healthcare organization. CAPriCORN represents 12 million+ patients who have sought care within the extended networks of their 10 

Page 19 

Facilities & Other Resources 



<!-- page 21 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

sites.  Demographics, disease conditions, and healthcare encounters are among the data points available to researchers and community members. A PopMedNet client was successfully installed and connected to the local research data mart. The research informatics group has data analysts with SAS and SQL capabilities along with appropriate licenses, and various health system leadership is eager to play a central role in CAPriCORN in order to advance patient centered comparative effectiveness research in Chicago and contribute to the National PCORnet infrastructure. They have shown their commitment with consistent presence at leadership meetings and by dedicating people and resources where needed to accomplish vital goals of CAPriCORN. 

### **The Department of Preventive Medicine** 

The Department of Preventive Medicine is comprised of multidisciplinary health scientists who focus on improving health and preventing disease across the life course in individuals and diverse populations. The Department of Preventive Medicine is comprised of 7 centers and 1 core, including the Biostatistics Collaboration Center, Center for Behavior and Health, Center for Behavioral Intervention Technologies, Center for Global Cardiovascular Health, Lurie Center for Global Oncology, Center for Population Health Sciences, the Data Analysis and Coordinating Center, and a Quantitative Data Sciences Core, of which will be available 

### Biostatistics Collaboration Center (BCC) 

The BCC supports investigators in the conduct of high-quality, innovative health-related research by providing expertise in biostatistics, statistical programming, and data management. The BCC consists of 11 faculty biostatisticians, seven statistical analysts, a financial administrator and an assistant research administrator. Center faculty and staff collaborate with investigators on all aspects of research: study design and grant development; data capture and statistical analysis; interpretation of results; and manuscript preparation. The Center contributes to all types of research, including basic science, clinical, epidemiological, and health services. The Biostatistics Collaboration Center is accessible to all researchers — from postdoctoral fellows to junior faculty and well-established investigators. The BCC provides Northwestern-affiliated investigators an initial one-hour consultation, subsidized by the Feinberg Research Office. Grant writing (e.g. developing analysis plans, sample size calculations) is also supported at no cost to full-time Feinberg faculty at the level of instructor or higher, with the goal of cultivating a collaborative environment and establishing relationships that will lead to successful grant applications.  When BCC faculty and staff help prepare and write grant applications, it is expected that the principal investigator will invite the BCC faculty to be the lead Biostatistician or Co-Investigator, and that a BCC statistical analyst will be included for % effort as appropriate. BCC involvement early on in the development of the application not only ensures that study design, power calculations and data analysis plans are appropriate and optimal, but also increases the chances of funding. The Biostatistics Collaboration Center maintains licenses for or provides access to many statistical and graphics packages including: Stata, Pass, Power and Precision, GraphPad Prism, Comprehensive Meta-Analysis, MATLAB, SAS/Windows, SPSS/Windows, R, RStudio, and REDCap. 

### **Northwestern University Institutional Review Board (IRB)** 

The Northwestern IRB meets the regulatory requirements found at 21 CFR 56.106 and 45 CFR 46.502. Northwestern has an active Federalwide Assurance (FWA number 00001549) with the Department of Health and Human Services, in which we promise to uphold federal regulations and ethical principles for human subjects research conducted by employees, agents, and affiliates of Northwestern.  Northwestern utilizes the electronic IRB (eIRB) system by Huron Click for routing, tracking, and the review and approval process for research studies submitted to the Northwestern IRB Office. 

<mark>COMPUTING AND INFORMATICS RESOURCES AT NORTHWESTERN UNIVERSITY</mark> 

### **High Performance Computing** 

The University's high performance computing (HPC) system is referred to as QUEST. Housed at the University's secure data center, it offers a large shared computational facility. Designed as a general use cluster, Quest supports the majority of HPC applications at Northwestern and is built to accommodate a wide variety of codes with great economy. NU researchers also have access to multiple regional super computers including Beagle and Beagle2 at Argonne National Laboratory. NU is part of The Great Lakes Consortium for Petascale Computation (GLCPC), a collaboration among colleges, universities, national research laboratories, and other educational institutions. The consortium facilitates the widespread and effective use of petascale computing, through the development of new computing software, applications, and 

Page 20 

Facilities & Other Resources 



<!-- page 22 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

technologies. A comprehensive educational and workforce development program ensures that advances made by consortium members are passed on to the next generation of researchers and applied to frontier research questions in science, technology, engineering, and the social sciences. The consortium is a key element of the **BLUE WATERS PROJECT** . The GLCPC has been allocated 3.5 million node hours (equivalent to approximately 50 million core hours) annually as part of the **BLUE WATERS PROJECT** . 

In 2010, the University launched Quest, a shared multidisciplinary High Performance Computing (HPC) facility and Vault which consists of over 15,000 cores and is a general use cluster. NUIT experienced a shift in grant funding support from approximately 30% to 80% in the past three years, which emphasizes the growing partnership between center and researcher in the use of shared services. Access free of charge is available in the form of testing accounts and research accounts. The latter is awarded on a quarterly basis through peer faculty reviews of allocation applications. Quest provides Northwestern faculty with a number of benefits including: 

- _Professional System Administration:_ NUIT HPC administrators handle security patches, operating system updates, hardware repairs, account management, and system troubleshooting so faculty and graduate students can focus on their research. 

- _Cost Effective:_ The NUIT data center provides infrastructure including floor space, cooling, power, software licenses for scheduling and management, core InfiniBand and Ethernet switching, storage nodes, management nodes, and login nodes. 

- _Application and Code Support:_ NUIT HPC Specialists help users with the installation, porting, and optimization of code on the Quest high performance computing system. 

Housed at the University's central data centers, these resources benefit from a secure, reliable, and stable environment and are designed to meet the computer and data storage needs of Northwestern University researchers. The Quest HPC system stands today at 7,056 compute cores hosting over 600 account holders representing 52 academic departments, over 190 research projects, and five schools. Six academic units have contributed funds to Quest in order to gain Full Access to the system and dedicated access to large volumes of storage. Technical staff hosts quarterly workshops for both new and current users of the University’s high performance computing system, Quest. Each course is designed to train researchers on how to use high performance computing and research storage resources for their research projects as well as to address individual questions and concerns. 

### **Research Storage** 

There are three main research data storage options for Northwestern researchers, depending on their storage needs and data requirements. These services include: Vault (Quest Storage), Research Data Storage Service Microsoft OneDrive, and SharePoint.. 

### Vault (Quest Storage) 

Vault is a central storage platform for research information that can work in tandem with Quest, the University’s central HPC system for research, to support Northwestern faculty research activities. Three types of storage services are available through the Vault platform: 

- Vault – Collaborative Research Storage: This is a Web-based tool for collaborative research data storage services., Users can upload, share and store research data such as datasets, proposals, digitized video, and images on their desktops via this secure, centralized repository. Research storage can be accessed through the Web and shared with internal and external collaborators for specified time periods. Each user receives 50 GBs of storage space at no charge and support to create project spaces upon request. 

- Vault – Supplementary Research Storage: This service is connected to Quest and select research instrumentation through lower-bandwidth IP networking. It is intended to facilitate data analysis pipelines, workflows, and meet the need for persistent storage of large-scale results from Quest computations. 

- Vault – HPC Project Storage: This service connects to Quest compute nodes through an InfiniBand fabric, enabling high-speed access to read and write data. Some research projects may require additional amounts of this highly connected storage to support research objectives. This option is offered when Vault – Supplementary Research Storage is not sufficient to support a particular research application. Vault – HPC Storage is allocated through project spaces available to Quest users.

This service provides a platform to store research data and is intended for storing files larger than 15 GB or when using regulated data, such as Protected Health Information (PHI) and Personally Identifiable Information (PII). ### <mark>The Research Data Storage Service</mark> 

<mark>The Research Data Storage provides campus researchers with a minimum of one Terabyte (TB) of desktop mountable storage for research purposes. This service provides a platform to store research data and is intended for storing files larger than 15 GB or when using regulated data, such as Protected Health Information (PHI) and Personally Identifiable Information (PII). The</mark> Research Data Storage Service may be used to store data that is subject to regulatory or compliance requirements, including but not limited to: HIPAA (Health Information Portability and Accountability Act), FISMA (Federal Information Security Management Act) and Personal Information Protection Act of Illinois. 

The Research Data Storage Service includes two storage zones based on data regulation guidelines: 1) Non-Audited – Appropriate for non-regulated research data, and 2) Audited – Appropriate for the storage of most legally and contractually restricted data, such as Personal Health Information (PHI) and Personally Identifiable Information (PII), or must be in compliance with regulations such as HIPAA and FISMA. Once storage has been allocated, campus researchers will access the service by mapping a network drive to the provisioned share. 

Key Research Data Storage Service Features and Benefits: 

- Compliant for storage for most legally or contractually restricted data. 

- Daily snapshots and data replication for disaster recovery saved for 28 days. 

- Auditing capabilities for reporting on access, creation, replication, updates, and deletion of files upon request. 

- Ability to share data with internal and external collaborators (via guest NetID). 

- Accessible via VPN when off campus. 

- Accepts common network drive mapping for Windows, Mac OS and Linux - SMB/CIFS access protocols. 

- Ability to manage user read-only and read/write access through established security groups using Outlook Web Access (OWA). 

### <mark>Microsoft OneDrive</mark> 

<mark>Microsoft OneDrive is a cloud-based storage platform that is apart of the Microsoft 365 applications. OneDrive allows files to be shared with other members of the university and has built-in security features including file encryption and data loss prevention.</mark> 

### Microsoft SharePoint 

<mark>SharePoint is a web-based platform, used as a place to store, access, share, and organize information and documents from multiple accounts. With SharePoint, files can be shared with internal and external users and lets simultaneous user collaboration on Office Documents.</mark> 

### **Networking Access** 

The network infrastructure for each NU school is connected by gigabit switches that are connected via gigabit fiber to the Northwestern backbone (10 gigabit or higher) and to Internet-2. Northwestern is a charter member of the Internet 2 consortium, and a member of the vBNS and MREN (the Chicago-area Metropolitan Research and Education Network, connecting the University of Chicago, Argonne National Laboratory, Fermi Laboratory, and the University of Illinois at Chicago at 10 gigabit or higher). Northwestern is the hub for StarLight, a worldwide high-speed optical research network, as well as a hub for much of the North American research Internet connectivity. This ideally situates Northwestern to host

Facilities & Other Resources
