Internet-based video conferencing sites and makes Northwestern a great location for housing world-class Internet applications. 

### **Central Visualization Facility** 

A 50 mega-pixel 3D-Stereo display wall is available to faculty and students via Northwestern Academic and Research Technologies (A&RT). This tiled wall display made up of 25 46” monitors capable of transforming research data into a 157 square foot, three-dimensional (3D) image, is located in Silverman Hall on the Northwestern Evanston Campus. 

### **Customer Support Services** 

Feinberg IT has a team of 19 people who provide technical support to Feinberg faculty and staff. The team provides phone and field support and has a centrally managed environment to ensure compliance with security measures such as software patching, encryption, and anti-virus software updates. Feinberg IT supports Macintosh and Windows computers equally across its 6,000 devices currently in the field. 

### **Infrastructure** 

The Feinberg IT Infrastructure team provides server, storage, and database administration support to Feinberg faculty and staff. Feinberg IT currently manages over 350 servers (both physical and virtualized) in the central NU data center located on the Northwestern Evanston campus. Feinberg currently has over 750 terabytes of storage across different platforms to serve the multifaceted needs of the medical school ranging from file shares to large research data sets to deep archive of legacy data. The Infrastructure team supports all custom-written software on a multitude of platforms including Python, Ruby on Rails, Java, .NET, ColdFusion, and many more. Tier 1 systems are mirrored in a disaster recovery data center on a separate campus, and all systems are backed up to multiple sites for recovery purposes. 

### **Data Security** 

Northwestern University (NU) and the Feinberg School of Medicine (FSM) have a formal information security program which includes a full-time Chief Information Security Officer (CISO) and security staff devoted to research programs conducted by FSM. FSM is further supported by a full-time CISO and security staff for NU. The FSM CISO works with CISOs of NU and its clinical partners to develop coordinated security policies. The FSM security team directly supports all human subject research programs within FSM. All human subject studies must submit a separate data security plan with is reviewed by security staff. 

### **Security Management** 

The NU information security program uses the Information Standards Organization’s (ISO) Standards for Security Management (27001) and Security Practice (27002) in support of the University’s Information Security Management System (ISMS). The NU information security program approach to National Institute for Standards and Technology (NIST)-based security projects embodies the process defined by NIST SP 800-18. Key deliverables include FIPS-199 Security Categorization, NIST SP 800-30 Risk Assessment, NIST SP 800-53r4 Information Security Plan, and an Authority to Operate Declaration. 

### **Secure Computing Environment** 

Northwestern University maintains a HIPAA compliant data center on the Evanston Campus. The Enterprise Data Warehouse infrastructure is located in the NMHC data center in Elk Grove Village, Illinois. These are connected to the Chicago campus by a redundant high-speed fiber-optic-cable-based Ethernet backbone and regular data backups. Failover between the campuses short of regional catastrophic failure will not jeopardize operations. All systems are monitored based on a tiered service availability model. Application servers and the database servers are built with high-availability and have failover capabilities that are tested frequently with minimal impact to end-users. We have multiple secure storage locations in different physical venues that are used to load and process/access research data in alternative locations. 

Page 23 

Facilities & Other Resources 



<!-- page 25 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

All users log in to campus computers using individually assigned user IDs and complex passwords through a single identity management system managed by Northwestern University. Off-campus access is permitted only through a secure VPN connection. Office facilities and physical file storage are in a secure building with a guard during regular business hours and key card access otherwise. 

### **Secure Server Data Storage** 

Research data is required to be stored on FSM servers/file storage managed centrally by FSM IT. Servers and file storage are located in secure HIPAA-ready data centers behind a secured tiered network infrastructure (e.g., firewalls) not directly exposed to the Internet. Specifically, these storage devices are Isilon storage with the 1FS operating system and mimic file directories in the Windows operating system as users are accustomed. Identifiable health/personal data used in research is required to be stored on secure/file storage. Access to project data is maintained consistent with a project-specific Authorized Personnel List as approved by the Northwestern University IRB. Data is replicated to an offsite server nightly. Server/file storage is managed by the central FSM IT department. 

### **Desktop and Laptop Security** 

Windows and Mac devices are configured, deployed, monitored, maintained, and patched by the central FSM IT department. All desktops, laptops and mobile devices are encrypted by policy. 

### **Email Security** 

FSM policy requires e-mail transmission of data to be encrypted. Email sent from @northwestern.edu e-mail addresses to recipients within Northwestern-affiliated domains are automatically encrypted. Messages containing PHI or PII data sent from a northwestern.edu e-mail address to non-Northwestern-affiliated environments will require manual encryption. Manually encrypted messages will be automatically quarantined. 

### **Research Application Design and Development (RADD)** 

The Research Application Design and Development (RADD) team provides ISGMH researchers technical services in order to design, develop, and implement effective research technologies and tools. RADD also provides expertise in recruitment and retention strategies to help researchers find participants to engage with their technical products and maintain their engagement. The team's services include development of software to be used within interventions, user interface and experience design, graphic design, wide-scale implementation strategy support, and social media recruitment campaign development. 

The RADD team has not only developed technologies to implement and support ISGMH’s various research initiatives, but has also developed new technologies for various Northwestern University collaborators and external academic collaborators. RADD Chat is a feature used to communicate with patients regarding recruitment and scheduling. 

### <mark>NU CLINICAL PARTNERS AND FACILITIES</mark> 

### **Northwestern Memorial HealthCare (NMHC)** 

Northwestern Memorial HealthCare (NMHC) is the corporate parent of 10 hospitals and 5 physician groups comprising over 1500 employed physicians, over 4600 affiliated physicians and 35,000 employees. NMHC has 2.6 million outpatient visits across 200+ sites per year. Northwestern Memorial Hospital (NMH), the academic flagship of this system, is one of the country’s premier academic hospitals and a national and international referral center. For the past 7 years, NMH has been recognized by _U.S. News & World Report_ as one of 17 top ‘Honor Roll’ hospitals, and in 2019, US News ranked NMH #10 in the United States. NMH is additionally recognized in the Best Hospitals ranking for outstanding performance in 11 of the 16 ranked specialties. NMH has a total of 894 licensed beds and houses the NUCATS Clinical Research Unit (CRU), a developmental therapeutics institute, and actively enrolls patients in every clinical department of the hospital. Clinical research studies at NMHC overall have grown from 2,100 in 2010 to 4,445 in 2017. The addition of Northwestern Medicine Central DuPage Hospital (NMCDH), Northwestern Medicine Delnor Hospital (NMDH), (formerly Cadence Health (including Central DuPage Hospital and Delnor Hospital and outpatient facilities and practices)) to the NMHC system promises to

provide even greater opportunities for clinical and translational research, with an additional 1 million patient visits per year. The growth of research is one of 3 pillars of the NMHC strategic plan. 

### **General Internal Medicine Outpatient Clinic (GIM)** 

The GIM Clinic of the Northwestern Memorial Faculty Foundation has 42 doctors who practice full or part time in the clinic. All physicians use an electronic medical record (Epic; Epic Systems Corporation; Madison, Wisconsin) for all clinical encounters (in-person and telephone). The GIM clinic was an early demonstration site for use of electronic medical records, and has been the site for several research studies of quality measurement and quality improvement pertaining to EMR use. The clinic is organized into four areas (“pods”) with separate nursing staff and physicians, creating an ideal environment for controlled clinical trials randomized at the pod level. A Patient Learning Center is located in the clinic building that provides patients with Internet access, videos, anatomical models to view and librarians to assist patients. The clinic provides over 60,000 visits per year for 25,000 patients, including 5,000 patients who are 65 years and older and taking 5 or more medications. The clinics of the Northwestern Medical Faculty Foundation serve a diverse patient population, which includes approximately 30% African American and 10% Latino patients and the entire spectrum of socioeconomic status. 

The GIM clinic has 1050 square feet of dedicated interview space for CAHRA, inclusive of nine private offices that are each equipped with locked storage, a desktop computer, high-speed internet and a phone. There is also a 150 square foot waiting area for participants to comfortably sit while waiting for their research interviews. The Division also has three dedicated examination rooms within the GIM clinic for research use and a Clinical Research Center with five examination rooms and one procedure room for biopsies in the same building. Within the Clinical Research Center is a separate, dedicated work station for research coordinators which consists of multiple desktop computers, phones and additional storage space.

**Equipment – Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai** 

### **<u>Computational Equipment</u>** 

**Institutional:** Mount Sinai’s computational and data science ecosystem which consists of high-performance computing (HPC) and the research clinical data warehouse. The HPC resource encompasses over 2 petaflops of computing and 28 petabytes of spinning storage. Minerva, created in 2012 and upgraded several times, most recently in 2021, contains over 20,000 Intel Gold 8168 24C, 2.7 GHz compute cores (48 cores per node with two sockets in each node) with 192 GB of memory per node, 210 terabytes of total memory, 350 terabytes of solidstate storage and 33 petabytes of usable, spinning storage accessed via IBM’s Spectrum Scale/General Parallel File System (GPFS). 48 NVIDIA V100 GPUs are available in 12 nodes and 32 NVIDIA A100 GPUs in 8 nodes, and 38 high memory nodes with 1.5 terabytes of memory each. All of the nodes are connected via a Mellanox Extended Data Rate (EDR) Infiniband fat tree fabric (100 Gb/s). 

The Mount Sinai Data Warehouse (MSDW) collects and organizes clinical, financial and operational data derived from patient care processes of the Mount Sinai Hospital and Mount Sinai Doctors Faculty Practice sourced from over 25 systems used at the Mount Sinai Hospital. MSDW contains over 4 billion facts and provides researchers access to over 9.3 million patient medical records including 45 million clinical documentation (progress notes, discharge summaries and operative reports), 107 million ICD-9 and ICD-10 coded diagnoses, 189 million number of medication (prescription and med-admins), 370 million test results (lab measurement, radiology, and pathology report) on 80 million number of patient visits. 1.5 million new facts are added every day. MSDW is compliant with New York State and HIPAA regulations and with Mount Sinai PPHS and IRB policies regarding protection of human subjects and participation in research. 

In 2019, the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai unveiled its new NIH-supported high-performance computing (HPC) cluster, Chimera. This resource is made available to NIH-funded researchers using high dimensional data and is supported by computational scientists and HPC administrators. Chimera includes 13,440 compute cores on 280 Intel Skylake compute nodes with additional high memory nodes and GPU compute nodes including 48 V100 GPUs. Chimera runs CentOS 7.6 Linux and supports Singularity containerization, which can run Docker images. Chimera also features 14 petabytes of file storage, a Globus endpoint for high performance file transfer, and a configurable offsite duplicated tape archive. The School’s NIH-funded researchers can also use a second dedicated cluster known as the Big Omics Data Engine (BODE2). Chimera allocations are available for all faculty research groups without charge and administered via the IBM LSF queuing software.

### **NIH BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH SUPPLEMENT** 

Name: Knobel, Pablo 

Persistent Identifier (PID) of the Senior/Key Person: <u>https://orcid.org/0000-0001-6902-0286</u> 

Position Title: Intructor 

Organization and Location: Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, New York, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am a Geospatial Epidemiologist in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai. My research examines how multidomain urban exposures influence cardiometabolic health and aging-related outcomes. My work encompasses the full analytic arc of environmental epidemiology: from geoprocessing novel exposure datasets and leveraging administrative and clinical cohorts to applying mixture-based statistical methods to understand how interacting environmental conditions shape human health. 

A substantial component of my research focuses on integrating high-resolution environmental data with large-scale electronic health records EHR. For example, in a New York City-based study, I investigated how varying dimensions of social environment relate to biological aging, demonstrating that social context exert distinct and measurable impacts on aging trajectories. I have also led analyses using comprehensive Mount Sinai EHR cohorts, World Trade Center responders, and Medicare, to evaluate the cumulative effects of multiple environmental exposures on cardiometabolic and aging outcomes, establishing the analytic and data infrastructure that underlies the current proposal. 

Beyond EHR-based studies, I lead and collaborate on urban exposome projects using diverse population datasets. Supported by the P30 HEALS pilot program, I am currently leading the "Tweets to Thoughts" project, which links bird biodiversity indicators to cognitive health in older adults across New York City—one of the first studies to integrate community-generated ecological signals with aging outcomes. I am also conducting a GECC-funded pilot project to map green space quality across the continental United States, generating a scalable, multidomain environmental dataset applicable to diverse aging cohorts. These projects illustrate my commitment to building rigorous, interoperable exposure datasets that can be applied across diverse clinical populations. 

A central methodological focus of my research is the application of multi-exposure statistical frameworks to environmental epidemiology. I have applied regression-based mixture approaches such as quantile G-computation (qGcomp), k-means clustering to generate urban nature exposure profiles, and non-negative matrix factorization (NMF) to decompose air pollution chemical components into emission sources. 

For the proposed R01, I will serve as Principal Investigator, leading an investigation into how the urban exposome shapes healthy aging across biological, physical, cognitive, psychological, and social domains. I will direct the full analytic arc of the project: geoprocessing and integrating the urban exposome across two longitudinal cohorts (the Mount Sinai Health System EHR, aproximately 20,000 patients in New York City, and LitCog, aproximately 900 participants in Chicago), applying mixture-based statistical frameworks including Exposome-Wide Association Study, quantile G-computation, and Bayesian Varying Coefficient Kernel Machine Regression, and leading the machine learning prediction framework that translates epidemiological findings into scenario-based projections of healthy aging outcomes under feasible urban interventions. 

I have not published under a different name. 

#### **Honors** 

2026 GeoCAFE Scholar, The BUSPH-HSPH CAFE Research Coordinating Center 2025 Best Poster Award , Santiago Exposome Symposium: Integrating Environmental Exposures into Research on Alzheimer’s Disease and Related Dementias

#### **Contributions to Science** 

1. Leveraging large-scale retrospective cohorts and Electronic Health Records (EHR) for environmental research: 

Modern epidemiologic research requires the integration of diverse clinical and administrative data streams to understand health impacts at a population scale. I have extensive experience defining cohorts and curating variables within complex health systems to evaluate environmental impacts on health. 

I have led analyses using the U.S. Medicare and World Trade Center (WTC) Responders cohorts to investigate the health impacts of air pollution. My work demonstrated significant associations between specific chemical components of air pollution and increased risks of CVD incidence, CVD mortality, and underlying cardiovascular risk factors. (Source-Specific PM(2.5) and Atherosclerotic Cardiovascular Disease Mortality, Ma, NEJM Evid, 2024.) 

I have worked directly with the Mount Sinai Health System EHR, linking patient residential addresses with high-resolution environmental data. This work spans the integration of multidomain exposures—from urban nature to social vulnerability— with longitudinal clinical data to evaluate biological aging and chronic disease trajectories. (Beyond greenness: multidimensional urban nature profiles and arteriosclerotic cardiovascular risk, Knobel, Environ Int, 2026) 

2. Investigating aging-related outcomes in older urban adults: 

The urban environment influences how people age. I have studied aging-related and cardiometabolic outcomes among older urban adults, linking high-resolution environmental exposures to biological aging and cardiovascular risk in large clinical cohorts. 

Using clinical laboratory data from the Mount Sinai EHR, I derived phenotypic age acceleration and linked it to neighborhood social conditions across NYC. I demonstrated that distinct dimensions of social context are each independently associated with accelerated biological aging, with effects that vary across dimensions rather than tracking a single axis (Social Vulnerability and Biological Aging in New York City: An Electronic Health Records-Based Study, Knobel, J Urban Health, 2025). C 

Complementary work examining cardiovascular disease among NYC residents older than 65 demonstrated that specific configurations of urban nature are independently protective, with residents in high-cover and waterfront profiles showing meaningfully lower cardiovascular risk than those in low-green environments (Beyond greenness: multidimensional urban nature profiles and arteriosclerotic cardiovascular risk, Knobel, Environ Int, 2026). 

3. Advancing multidimensional assessment of urban nature for epidemiology: 

Urban nature exposures are frequently treated as single-dimensional metrics (e.g., presence vs. absence of greenness), which overlooks the critical heterogeneity in quality and functionality that drives health outcomes. To address this gap, I lead a research program focused on building high-resolution, multidomain datasets that move the field toward more granular metrics of environmental exposure and their direct impact on health outcomes. 

NIH Biographical Sketch v.2026-1 

Page 35 

Biosketches 



<!-- page 37 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

I designed and led the development of RECITAL, a comprehensive tool for evaluating urban green space quality. By fieldtesting this tool across more than 200 sites and generating over 90 quality indicators, I demonstrated that different greenness attributes (e.g., amenities, safety, and biodiversity) have distinct and sometimes divergent associations with physical activity, obesity, and cardiometabolic risk factors (Quality of urban green spaces influences residents' use of these spaces, physical activity, and overweight/obesity, Knobel, Environ Pollut, 2021.) 

In my Beyond Greeness research, I utilized eight distinct metrics of urban nature—spanning greenness, blue space, and biodiversity—to create complex environmental profiles across the urban landscape. I assessed the association between these multidimensional profiles and Atherosclerotic Cardiovascular Disease (ASCVD), illustrating that specific configurations of urban nature provide different levels of cardiovascular protection. (Beyond greenness: multidimensional urban nature profiles and arteriosclerotic cardiovascular risk, Knobel, Environ Int, 2026.) 

4. Advancing New York City (NYC) epidemiology through high-resolution exposure science: 

The majority of my research program is centered on NYC, where I have advanced the field of exposure assessment by characterizing the city's unique social and built environmental complexities. My work provides a deeper understanding of the specific urban determinants that drive health disparities in dense metropolitan areas. 

I led an investigation into the relationship between social vulnerability and phenotypic age within NYC. By utilizing highresolution neighborhood data, I demonstrated that distinct dimensions of social adversity are significantly associated with accelerated biological aging, highlighting the specific urban social structures that impact the aging process (Social Vulnerability and Biological Aging in New York City: An Electronic Health Records-Based Study, Knobel, J Urban Health, 2025.). 

My studies on NYC's urban nature have contributed to a better understanding of how green and blue spaces are distributed across the five boroughs. This work identifies inequities in access to high-quality natural environments and provides the geospatial evidence needed to evaluate how these distributions correlate with population health outcomes (Beyond greenness: multidimensional urban nature profiles and arteriosclerotic cardiovascular risk, Knobel, Environ Int, 2026.).

### **NIH BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH SUPPLEMENT** 

Name: Yitshak Sade, Maayan 

Persistent Identifier (PID) of the Senior/Key Person: <u>https://orcid.org/0000-0003-4453-0401</u> 

Position Title: Associate Professor 

Organization and Location: Department of Environmental Medicine, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, New York, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am an environmental epidemiologist and Associate Professor in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai. My research focuses on the complex relationships between environmental and built-environment exposures and aging-related outcomes, including cognitive decline, dementia, frailty, and cardiometabolic disease. I employ rigorous epidemiologic methods and advanced statistical modeling across large, diverse populations, with expertise in electronic health records (EHRs) and large-scale cohorts. I lead and collaborate on large-scale epidemiologic studies using U.S. nationwide Medicare data, EHRs, and prospective and registry-based cohorts to examine how environmental factors influence morbidity, hospital admissions, and mortality. 

A central focus of my work is disentangling complex exposure mixtures and identifying particularly harmful chemical components to inform public health interventions and policy. I was awarded the NIEHS Outstanding New Environmental Scientist award (R01 ES034864-01) and a KL2 award (KL2TR004421) to investigate World Trade Center (WTC)-related exposures and subsequent environmental exposures in relation to cardiometabolic health among general responders. This work has resulted in multiple publications demonstrating increased cardiometabolic risk associated with exposure to an extreme environmental disaster and subsequent air pollution mixtures. We showed that WTC-related exposures were associated with worsening trajectories of systolic blood pressure and glucose, and that later air pollution exposures further contributed to adverse cardiometabolic outcomes, with specific pollution sources driving these risks. Importantly, in a preliminary analysis, we identified heightened vulnerability to airpollution mixture effects among individuals with the most intense WTC exposures, underscoring the need to evaluate cumulative environmental exposures in disaster-exposed populations. These findings have significant public health relevance, as empirical evidence informs decisions regarding coverage of 9/11-related health conditions and advances the broader field of environmental disaster research. 

I also led a national study of more than 30 million Medicare beneficiaries that examined source-specific PM₂.₅ and cardiovascular disease mortality among older adults, recently published in NEJM Evidence, providing population-level evidence that PM₂.₅ toxicity varies by chemical composition and emission source. In parallel, I am investigating environmental exposure mixtures and ischemic stroke risk in a national study in Israel (U.S.–Israeli Binational Science Foundation, grant 2021197), leveraging high-resolution exposure models and clinical data. Additionally, Drs. Hung, Kloog, and I received a P30 pilot grant (P30ES023515 AND P30AG021342) to study environmental risk factors for Alzheimer’s disease and related dementias (ADRD) among U.S. Veterans, focusing on cumulative and mixture-based exposures in a high-risk population. 

I have a long history of collaboration with Dr. Knobel and am excited to contribute my input and expertise on air pollution exposure mixture health effects among older adults. 

I have not published or created research products under another name. 

#### **Honors** 

2025 Robin Chemers Neustein Mid-Career Faculty Award, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai 2023 Selected for induction as a faculty member, The Delta Omega, the Public Health Honorary Society 2014 John Goldsmith Award in Environmental Epidemiology for Master Students, Ben Gurion University

#### **Contributions to Science** 

1. Advancing understanding of environmental mixtures and health, with a focus on fine particulate matter (PM₂.₅) as a complex mixture of chemical components: 

My work has advanced the conceptualization of PM₂.₅ as a heterogeneous mixture of chemical components with distinct health effects, moving beyond traditional single-pollutant approaches. I have led and contributed to studies that integrate highresolution exposure models with large-scale epidemiologic data to identify biologically relevant PM₂.₅ mixtures associated with cardiometabolic and cerebrovascular outcomes. 

In Ma et al. (2024, NEJM Evidence), we demonstrated that source-specific PM₂.₅ components were differentially associated with atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease mortality among older adults, providing direct evidence that PM₂.₅ toxicity depends on its chemical and source composition. Complementary work linked long-term exposure to specific PM₂.₅ constituents with ischemic stroke risk in the New York City metropolitan area (Krasnov et al., 2025, Chemosphere), emphasizing the importance of investigating mixture-specific associations. This extends earlier work that showed associations between ambient PM₂.₅ levels and mortality, highlighting the public health relevance of air quality regulation (Yitshak-Sade et al., 2019, Environmental Epidemiology). 

In summary, these studies establish a strong foundation for investigating PM₂.₅ as a multidimensional exposure and motivate mixture-based approaches to environmental health research. 

2. Characterizing the long-term health impacts of environmental disasters, with a focus on the World Trade Center (WTC): 

I have contributed to understanding how acute environmental disasters interact with chronic environmental exposures to influence long-term cardiometabolic health. My work within the World Trade Center (WTC) Health Program has helped characterize the enduring health consequences of complex, high-intensity disaster-related exposures among responders. In Krasnov et al. (2025, American Journal of Public Health), we demonstrated that WTC exposures were associated with elevated cardiometabolic risk among general responders, providing evidence of persistent health effects decades after the disaster (Knobel et al, 2024, American Journal of Epidemiology). Extending this work, Krasnov et al. (2025, American Journal of Epidemiology) showed that long-term exposure to specific PM₂.₅ sources was associated with increased incidence of diabetes and higher blood glucose levels among WTC responders, highlighting the role of source-specific air pollution in post-disaster metabolic dysfunction. Complementary evidence from Knobel et al. (2024, American Journal of Epidemiology) demonstrated that an extreme air pollution event, superimposed on two decades of moderate exposure, was associated with elevated glucose and lipid levels, reinforcing the importance of cumulative exposure frameworks. 

In preliminary analyses, we identified heightened vulnerability to air pollution mixture effects among individuals with the most intense WTC exposures, underscoring the need to evaluate cumulative environmental exposures in disaster-exposed populations. 

Together, these studies provide a conceptual basis for viewing disaster-related exposures as part of a cumulative environmental exposome rather than isolated events, with implications for long-term surveillance, risk stratification, and intervention among disaster-exposed populations. 

3. Elucidating the role of environmental exposures in aging, cognitive decline, and Alzheimer’s disease, and related dementias (ADRD): 

NIH Biographical Sketch v.2026-1 

Page 40 

Biosketches 



<!-- page 42 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

My research has substantially contributed to understanding how environmental exposures influence aging-related outcomes, particularly among vulnerable older adults and individuals with neurodegenerative disease. Using large national cohorts and electronic health records, I have examined how air pollution contributes to disease progression, hospitalization, and mortality in aging populations. In Dong et al. (2025, The Lancet Planetary Health), we demonstrated that air pollution exposure was associated with increased mortality and hospital readmissions among Medicare beneficiaries with Alzheimer’s disease and related dementias (ADRD), identifying this population as highly susceptible to environmental stressors. 

Earlier work showed that PM₂.₅ exposure increased hospital admissions among Medicare enrollees with chronic debilitating brain disorders (Yitshak-Sade et al., 2021, Science of the Total Environment), providing early evidence of heightened vulnerability in neurologically impaired populations. In addition, we identified synergistic effects between short-term temperature and PM₂.₅ exposures on hospital admissions, demonstrating that environmental stressors do not act independently (Yitshak-Sade et al., 2018, Science of the Total Environment). 

Our previous studies support the hypothesis that environmental exposures accelerate aging processes and worsen cognitive and functional outcomes, directly motivating our current work on air pollution mixtures and ADRD. 

4. Advancing exposomic research in aging populations: 

I contribute to exposomic research by developing integrative frameworks that capture the cumulative and multidimensional nature of environmental exposures. Rather than evaluating isolated exposures, my research emphasizes the joint effects of air pollution mixtures, weather, neighborhood environments, and extreme events to better reflect the complexity of real-world exposure. 

Across multiple studies, I have applied high-resolution geospatial exposure models to large-scale cohorts to characterize how combinations of environmental stressors interact over time to influence health outcomes. For example, I demonstrated that cooccurring environmental exposures act synergistically to increase hospital admissions (Yitshak-Sade et al., 2018, Science of the Total Environment). My work on disaster-exposed populations further shows that high-intensity WTC-related exposures contribute to adverse cardiometabolic outcomes independently of subsequent air pollution mixtures (Krasnov et al., 2025, American Journal of Public Health; Krasnov et al., 2025, American Journal of Epidemiology). In addition, I have expanded exposomic research beyond pollutants to include multidimensional neighborhood environments and identified urban nature profiles that are differentially associated with cardiovascular risk (Knobel et al., 2025, Environment International). Complementary mixture-based analyses demonstrate that PM₂.₅ health effects depend on chemical composition and source (Ma et al., 2024, NEJM Evidence; Krasnov et al., 2025, Chemosphere). 

Building on this work, our preliminary exposomic analyses integrating air pollution, temperature, and greenness identified significant associations between combined exposure profiles and frailty and mental health among WTC responders. Together, these studies advance exposomic approaches with direct relevance to research on aging and Alzheimer’s disease and related dementias (ADRD).

Name: Kloog, Itai 

Persistent Identifier (PID) of the Senior/Key Person: <u>https://orcid.org/0000-0003-1708-7440</u> 

Position Title: Professor 

Organization and Location: Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, New York, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am a health geographer and Professor in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai. My work lies at the intersection of environmental health, geostatistics, GIS, and remote sensing, where I have established myself as a global leader in developing and applying innovative spatial methods to solve core challenges in exposure science. 

My research focuses on advancing modeling techniques to resolve environmental exposures at high temporal and spatial resolutions. I have led multiple NIH and EU-funded projects that have enabled the development of next-generation exposure models. These models, published in leading journals, represent a significant advancement over previous “state-of-the-art” methods and have made it possible to conduct novel epidemiological studies that were previously infeasible. Building on this foundation, I utilize advanced machine learning architectures, including random forests and deep learning frameworks, to address complex prediction-type questions in environmental health. This includes the algorithmic development of ensemble learners that integrate multi-source big data to improve the predictive accuracy of exposure estimates in areas with sparse monitoring. 

A central contribution of my lab has been the fusion of satellite-based data streams with geostatistical algorithms to extend the spatiotemporal reach of environmental exposure estimates. As an exposure scientist, geo-statistician, and GIS specialist, I create highresolution ambient exposure models using hybrid approaches that integrate remote sensing, geospatial datasets, and public data sources such as USGS, NASA, TIGER, Twitter, census data, and health registries. 

My lab’s work supports epidemiologic investigations by developing ambient exposure models for air pollution, temperature extremes, and built environment metrics. We specialize in GIS and remote sensing, spatial and geostatistical algorithm development, and quantifying the health impacts of environmental exposures. 

I have published extensively on spatially and temporally resolved exposure assessment for fine particulate matter (PM2.5) and air temperature, with applications across the continental U.S. and globally. These models have been critical inputs for numerous cohort studies, including high-impact analyses of environmental exposures and adverse health outcomes. In particular, our models have supported key environmental epidemiology and NIH ECHO studies linking air pollution and temperature exposures to a range of health endpoints. 

I have not published or created research products under any other name. 

#### **Honors** 

2020 Strieg Excellence Award in Environmental Sciences, Ben Gurion University 2010 EHF Postdoctoral distinguished Fellowship, Environmental Health Fund 2006 Excellent Thesis Rector Award, Haifa University 

#### **Contributions to Science** 

NIH Biographical Sketch v.2026-1 

Page 45 

Biosketches 



<!-- page 47 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

1. Air pollution exposure science using remote sensing and GIS: Air pollution is a threat to human health with multiple adverse health effects. Most studies on the health effects of air pollution are conducted over large geographical areas as the unit of analysis (country wide), thus potentially resulting in inaccurate exposure-outcome associations due to exposure measurement error (i.e., assigning inaccurate exposure to each study participant due to limited spatial exposure data). These studies have traditionally focused on urban areas, since air pollution monitors are placed mostly in major cities and the bulk of the population is concentrated there. To address these limitations, I develop approaches which substantially outperform the ‘stateof-the-art’ current air pollution estimation models used by other groups. The novel modeling approach I developed bridges the methodological and knowledge gaps that needed to be filled to: 1. Address exposure misclassification issues which results inaccurate health effect estimates due to spatial misalignment; and 2. Better identify the sources of heterogeneity in the short and long term exposure effects to air pollution across large territories and sub-populations. The novelty in my approach lies in the ability to develop new satellite-based GIS and geo-statistical methodologies extending the spatiotemporal scale dramatically. 

2. Spatiotemporal exposure modeling for temperature and other factors: Just as current epidemiological studies are addressing health risks from environmental PM pollution, they are also now starting to address the health impact of exposure to other important environmental exposures such as extreme air temperature events, green space availability, Light at Night exposure and many other environmental factors. To robustly asses these exposures, I focus on the challenges of developing robust highresolution environmental exposure assessment models and studying more accurate exposure-health outcome associations in environmental health studies. I apply advanced geo-statistical methods and novel GIS/remote sensing-based technologies to address these scientific challenges and resolve these environmental exposures temporally and spatially. 

3. Data mining, Social media and built environment exposures: While considerable attention has been paid to the public-healthrelated impacts of classic exposers such as air pollution and temperature, relatively little research has been done to understand how other aspects of the built environment and neighborhoods impact health. I focus on developing novel built environment exposure assessment models by developing and applying new data mining algorithms, advanced web scraping tools based on LBSN (location based social networks), image processing from LBSN services, in-situ monitoring campaigns and tying all these resulting rich datasets with cutting edge geo-statistical methods to address these new scientific challenges. We develop among others walkability measures, noise exposure estimates, sentiment scores and wellbeing indexes based on our new methodologies. 

4. Reproducible science and Open source tools: The contemporary scientific community places a growing emphasis on both the reproducibility of research and the dissemination of tools and methods developed for increased collaboration, reproducibility and advancement of science. Having all tools, code and methods open sourced and freely available (FLOSS) will improve the reliability and efficiency of scientific research, increase the credibility of published scientific literature and accelerate discovery across the broader research community. The common method these days for such dissemination is to use online code and data repositories. Fundamental open source Tools such as git, github, R (R Foundation for Statistical Computing, Vienna, Austria), knitr, Python, Docker, ORCID are all commonly used today in the research community across all disciplines and in all out labs and research in our group. We adhere to this philosophy and provide examples of shared resources below.

### **NIH BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH SUPPLEMENT** 

Name: Midya, Vishal 

Persistent Identifier (PID) of the Senior/Key Person: <u>https://orcid.org/0000-0002-6643-5176</u> 

Position Title: Assistant Professor 

Organization and Location: ICAHN SCHOOL OF MEDICINE AT MOUNT SINAI, New York, New York, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am an Assistant Professor at the Department of Environmental Medicine (with joint affiliation with the Department of Public Health) at Mount Sinai, with expertise in developing and applying machine learning models for precision environmental health research. My primary research focuses on understanding (1) how prenatal and early-life exposure to environmental chemicals affects childhood neurodevelopmental outcomes, (2) intestinal inflammation and gut microbial diversity, which may lead to adverse health outcomes over the life course, and (3) identifying metabolic signatures of elemental biodynamics that can predict long-term health outcomes. I specifically use interpretable machine learning techniques to discover such environmental signals. My research has broadly shown that (1) early-life environmental metal exposures may be associated with childhood gut microbial dysbiosis and gut inflammation. (2) Using a random forest-based interpretable machine learning algorithm, we have devised an algorithm to search for high-dimensional exposure signatures. (3) We developed architectures for identifying signatures of synchronicity in elemental biodynamics based on hair and teeth elemental data. 

In this novel R01 with Dr. Knobel, I will leverage my biostatistical and data science skills to model urban exposure mixtures and aging outcomes. I have considerable expertise in exposure mixture models and high-dimensional analysis, particularly in Bayesian model architectures as well as in exposome-wide analyses. I have also implemented and supervised the end-to-end construction and deployment of a non-leakage exposome-based predictive model. Given this biostatistical expertise and the intersection of these areas of interest, I can serve as a Co-I and lead the biostatistical component of this project. 

I confirm that I have not published under a different name. 

#### **Honors** 

|2024|Invited speaker at CDC’s third annual environmental epidemiology methods workshop, CDC: Centers for Disease<br>Control and Prevention|
|---|---|
|2022|Lilienfeld Postdoctoral Prize Paper Award Finalist, Society for Epidemiologic Researc|
|2019|Ranked 3rd worldwide in Solar Flare Prediction Model, Kaggle BigData Cup Challenge|
|2018|Graduate Alumni Society Award, Penn State College of Medicine|
|2014|All India Rank 22nd in Mathematics Statistics, Indian Institute of Technology- Joint Admission test for Masters<br>(IIT JAM)|
|2014|Summer Research Fellowship in Mathematics, Indian Academy of Sciences (IASc)/Indian National Science<br>Academy (INSA)/ The National Academy of Sciences (NASI)|
|2011 - 2016|Kishore Vaigyanik Protsahan Yojana (KYPY) Fellowship, Department of Science and Technology, Government<br>of India|



#### **Contributions to Science** 

1. Interpretable Machine Learning and Exposure Mixture Models: 

I developed novel penalized hierarchical Bayesian model exposure mixture models to identify chemical group-specific effects as well as overall effects. My research has focused on combining interpretable machine-learning models with chemical mixture models to understand high-dimensional interactions that are only present in specific subpopulations. I introduced a combination of classical exposure-mixture Weighted Quantile Sum regression and machine-learning methods to identify synergistic interactions among environmental chemicals. To understand the basis of inter-individual differences in susceptibility, I have introduced chemical clique signatures for precision environmental health, which characterize a specific subset of the population. 

NIH Biographical Sketch v.2026-1 

Page 49 

Biosketches 



<!-- page 51 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

2. Environmental Chemical Exposome and Microbiome: 

In this relatively newer field of gut microbial exposomics, I study the effect of early-life metals on childhood gut microbial composition. Using machine-learning models and inferential tools, I developed statistical methods to identify local relationships between multiple bacteria (or microbial clique), and how early-life exposures to metals may disrupt these local group dynamics. Results from these methodologies carry the potential for targeted intervention. 

3. Environmental Chemical Exposures and Gastrointestinal Health: 

Due to my interest in microbial exposomics, I have recently initiated a research portfolio focused on the intersection of exposomics and gastrointestinal health. Specific developmental etiologies of Inflammatory Bowel Disease (IBD) are still unknown. Research suggests that the developmental origins of IBD may be heterogeneous. My current research focuses on understanding this heterogeneous impact of exposomics on the origins of IBD from the lens of precision environmental health. 

4. Elemental Biodynamics in Health: 

In this relatively new approach, I analyzed the dynamic patterns and synchronicity of elements over time in hair strands/teeth as biomarkers for systemic health, particularly for diseases such as ALS or Autism. I employ complex system analysis techniques to investigate the dynamics of time-varying interactions between essential and toxic elements within our bodies. moving beyond single-time point measurements to predict risks of neurodevelopmental or neurodegenerative disorders.

### **NIH BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH SUPPLEMENT** 

Name: Federman, Alex D 

Persistent Identifier (PID) of the Senior/Key Person: <u>https://orcid.org/0000-0002-4824-064X</u> 

Position Title: Professor of Medicine 

Organization and Location: Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, New York, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am a primary care physician and clinical scientist, Professor of Medicine in the Departments of Medicine and Geriatrics and Palliative Medicine, Director for Research in the Division of General Internal Medicine, and Program Director for the NIA Aging & Dementia T32 post-doctoral research training program. My research focuses on issues that complicate healthcare and health outcomes for older adults with complex chronic illness, with a special focus on the role of cognitive impairment. Specifically, I have studied cognitive factors that affect the ability of older adults to effectively and successfully manage their chronic illnesses, such as asthma and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. In more recent work, I am studying prevalence of cognitive impairment among older adults in primary care settings and developing new methods to screen for cognitive impairment in primary care, including machine learning applied to electronic medical data. Additionally, I lead the primary care roll out of the Medicare Guiding an Improved Dementia Care Experience (GUIDE) demonstration in the Mount Sinai Health System. These experiences position me well to contribute to Dr. Knobel’s R01 proposal by providing insights on dementia detection and management in outpatient healthcare settings. I have collaborated with Dr. Knobel on multiple projects resulting in 4 published papers. 

I confirm that I have not published or created any research products under another name. 

#### **Honors** 

- 2024 Tenure, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai 2022 Health and Aging Policy Fellow, US Senate Special Committee on Aging 2022 Senior Faculty Award, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai 2012 Master Researcher, Society of General Internal Medicine Mid-Atlantic Regional Meeting 2011 Clifford L. Spingarn, MD Professor in Primary Care Medicine, Endowed Professorship, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai 

- 2010 Department of Medicine Research Excellence Award, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai 2006 Paul B. Beeson Scholar Award in Aging Research, National Institute on Aging 2004 Generalist Physicians Faculty Scholar, Robert Wood Johnson 1996 Outstanding Senior Resident, Department of Medicine, Montefiore Medical Center 

#### **Contributions to Science** 

1. My research focuses on aging, cognition, and dementia assessment in primary care settings, with particular attention to how cognitive impairment affects chronic disease management and health outcomes in older adults (PMID: 33249956). Recently, we have begun examining prevalence of cognitive impairment in primary care settings in preparation for interventions to improve screening for cognitive impairment for older adults. 

2. I have also examined various aspects of home-based care delivery for older adults. This work includes examinations of the outcomes of hospital at home for Medicare beneficiaries, home-based primary care for older homebound adults, and chronic illness self-management in the home for older adults (PMID: 36054295). 

3. My early work focused on access to affordable health care coverage by older adults and implications of that coverage. Older adults have had limited access to complete coverage of their healthcare needs. In several studies, I have reported low rates of participation in programs designed to provide extended coverage for low income older adults who do not qualify for full coverage Medicaid, and demonstrated that absence of this coverage is associated with lower rates of use of important medical services. 

NIH Biographical Sketch v.2026-1 

Page 53 

Biosketches 



<!-- page 55 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

4. Much my work in recent years has focused on self-management behaviors of older adults with chronic illness, specifically asthma and COPD. I was PI on the largest longitudinal cohort study of older asthmatics in the U.S. This work showed close associations between psychosocial factors (cognition, health literacy, illness related beliefs) and poor asthma self-management behaviors, including medication adherence, as well as with asthma outcomes like hospitalization (PMID: 27555454). It has also examined the role of symptom perception in self-care among older adults with asthma. We applied this work to the development of asthma and COPD self-management support interventions led by community health workers. 

5. I have conducted several trials of interventions to improve outcomes for older adults with asthma, COPD and complex chronic illness including the Support self-Management Behaviors in older Adults (SAMBA) model. I have also studied different models of health care delivery in the home for older adults, including home-based primary care, hospital at home, and homebased self-management support (PMID: 36054295).

### **NIH BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH SUPPLEMENT** 

Name: WOLF, MICHAEL SCOTT 

Persistent Identifier (PID) of the Senior/Key Person: <u>https://orcid.org/0000-0002-4342-6517</u> 

Position Title: James R. Webster, Jr. Professor of Medicine; Director, Center for Applied Health Research on Aging; Associate Division Chief (General Internal Medicine); Associate Vice Chair (Medicine) 

Organization and Location: Northwestern University, Chicago, Illinois, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am a health services researcher and cognitive-behavioral scientist with expertise in cognitive aging, medication safety and adherence, health literacy, chronic disease self-management, and health system interventions to promote health, particularly in diverse clinical settings and among medically complex older adults living with multiple chronic conditions. (H Index: 96 (Google Scholar), 78 (Scopus), 75 (Web of Science). For the past two decades, I have led: 1) observational studies investigating individual, health system, and community-level determinants of health services use, health status, and clinical outcomes and known disparities; and 2) multi-site pragmatic clinical trials testing the effectiveness and fidelity of community and health system-based interventions that leverage health and consumer technologies to improve healthcare quality, safety, and equity through improved patient/consumer engagement and care management. 

In 2004, I founded the Health Literacy & Learning Program to create a network of multidisciplinary faculty that would generate applied research to transform the delivery of healthcare and public health for more vulnerable patients. Since its inception, I have received >$120 million in grant funding. In 2019, I was named Founding Director, with an institutional endowment, of the Feinberg School of Medicine’s Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA). CAHRA seeks to promote optimal health and wellbeing among individuals and families over the life span. My other roles include being contact PI and Co-Director of Northwestern’s NIA-sponsored Claude D. Pepper Older Americans Independence Center (OAIC, P30AG059988) and administrative roles as Associate Division Chief (General Internal Medicine) and Vice Chair of Research (Department of Medicine). Since its inception in 2017, I have been a steering committee member of NINDS’ Consortium for Detecting Cognitive Impairment, including Dementia (DetectCID), and also recently served on the NINDS 2022 ADRD Summit Multiple Etiology Dementias (MED) subcommittee. 

I currently lead 3 NIA cohort studies of middle-aged and older adult primary care patients (R01AG030611; R01AG070212; R01AG075043) that investigate cognitive trajectories and modifiable factors that could mitigate more rapid declines in health. In addition, I am contact PI (U01NS105562) of a U01 pragmatic trial (‘MyCog’) that addresses early detection of cognitive impairment including dementias in primary care settings serving health disparate populations. Our MyCog assessment has been piloted among 46,000+ patients in the Oak Street Health across clinics in the midwest and southeast US. 

#### **Honors** 

2019 James R. Webster, Jr. Endowed Chair in Medicine, Northwestern University, Department of Medicine 2010 - 2011 J. William Fulbright Distinguished Faculty Fellowship (United Kingdom), Fulbright Scholars Program 2009 Advancing Health Literacy and Patient Safety Award, National Patient Safety Foundation 2008 Pfizer Clear Health Communication Visiting Professor Award , New York University 2007 Department of Medicine Investigators Developing Excellence in Academics (IDEAS) Award, Northwestern University 

#### **Contributions to Science** 

1. <u>Cognitive and Psychosocial Determinants of Health.</u> For the past 2 decades, I have been involved in cognitive aging investigations that have identified individual and health system factors predicting outcomes. In particular, my research has shown that patients experiencing cognitive decline have poorer health literacy, higher rates of chronic conditions and activity limitations, lower mean physical function, and higher adjusted relative risk of cardiovascular death. 

NIH Biographical Sketch v.2026-1 

Page 57 

Biosketches 



<!-- page 59 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

2. <u>Health Literacy & Self-Management Capacity.</u> According to a 2019 National Library of Medicine report, I have led peerreviewed publications of health literacy studies, which examine individual proficiencies with regard to accessing healthcare services and products, and managing personal health. My research highlights the importance of health systems to address treatment adherence with individuals for all of their chronic conditions and working with them to develop and implement strategies to self-manage medications effectively. My work in this area has been widely disseminated to the public through 50+ state and national news media outlets (Altmetric.com). 

3. <u>Medication Labeling and Safe Use.</u> Arguably the most common and demanding self-care task for managing chronic conditions is taking medication. I have focused both on medication safety and adherence in seeking to understand how adults (as parents and patients) organize and take prescribed medication to inform health system responses. My work has led to a new evidencebased practice standard (Universal Medication Schedule) for how to prescribe medications in a manner that can support patient understanding, reduce unintentional misuse, and promote adherence. I have extensive experience leading analyses using several large EHR datasets and pharmacy claims (N>600,000) and am able to mentor Dr. Russell on these methods. This research has been the subject of numerous FDA, Brookings Institution, U.S. Pharmacopeia, American College of Physicians working groups and has been presented internationally. 

4. <u>Disparities and Design of Health Technologies to Support Self-Care.</u> EHR tools and mHealth technology are increasingly becoming the standard for communicating with patients and monitoring their conditions. I have focused on how older adults adopt these technologies, and work with EHR vendors, payers, pharmacy chains, and diverse health systems to design tools to prompt prescriber counseling during encounters, automate the generation of useful after visit summaries, and impart patient portal tools, interactive voice recognition software, and SMS text platforms to continually engage patients. 

5. <u>Healthcare System Approaches to Promote Patient Engagement and Outcomes.All of the observational and developmental</u> work that I have done culminates in the ability to inform the design of practical, sustainable, and ultimately scalable health system strategies to support patients and families. I continue to lead several large, NIH and privately-funded pragmatic trials testing scalable approaches to promote healthcare quality, safety and equity by nudging providers and patients beyond the point of care, onboard them to available technology platforms, promote recommended behaviors, monitor safety, and mobilize care teams if issues arise.

Organization and Location: Northwestern University, Chicago, Illinois, United States 

#### **Personal Statement** 

I am an Assistant Professor in the Division of General Internal Medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, where I co-lead the Measurement and Analysis Program (MAP) within the Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA). The MAP, comprised of three faculty members and six statistical analysts, provides expertise in biostatistical science, programming, and data management to the aging research done by CAHRA investigators, including study design and grant development, data capture and statistical analysis, interpretation of results, and manuscript preparation. As a member of this program for nearly a decade, I bring both extensive statistical and epidemiological expertise and a passion for improving health outcomes for middle-aged and older adults. 

My portfolio includes a wide range of collaborative research activities, from grant development to managing complex statistical analyses. I have served as a Co-Investigator on several cohort studies involving health literacy and cognitive function among middle age and older adults (R01AG070212, R01AG030611), as well as self-management of chronic conditions (R01AG075043). As the lead analyst on these studies, I have utilized a variety of analytic methods, including mixed models, trajectory analysis, random forest, and factor analysis, to answer the complex research questions proposed in these cohorts. These analytic methods can be directly applied to the analysis plan developed in the Urban Exposome and Healthy Aging grant proposal. 

Further, I have extensive experience analyzing blood biomarker data, including DNA methylation and gene expression, in the Health and Retirement Study (HRS), a nationally representative longitudinal study of adults over age 50 that assesses metrics of physical and mental health, and wealth. My research primarily focuses on understanding the complex relationship between psychosocial factors and epigenetics, including biological age acceleration, and how these interactions influence chronic diseases and cognitive function. This work bridges several scientific disciplines to examine how environmental and social stressors can alter gene expression and, in turn, affect disease susceptibility and cognitive outcomes. 

For this study, I will serve as a co-investigator to support The Urban Exposome and Healthy Aging: From Evidence to Intervention Projections Across Two US Cities. I will work closely with Dr. Knobel (PI), leveraging my expertise in cognitive aging, biological aging research, and longitudinal data analysis. I have a proven track record of conducting successful and impactful research, and I am delighted to have the opportunity to continue to support this study. 

**Ongoing and recently completed projects that I would like to highlight include:** 

R01AG070212 Wolf (PI), Role: Co-Investigator 03/2020-02/2025 Health Literacy and Cognitive Function among Middle-Aged Adults: The MidCog Study 

R01AG030611 Wolf (PI), Role: Co-Investigator 03/2020-08/2029 LitCog IV: Health Literacy and Cognitive Function among Older Adults 

R01AG075043 Wolf (PI), Role: Co-Investigator 06/2022-03/2027 Long-Term Impact of the COVID-19 Pandemic on Self-Management of Chronic Conditions: The C3 Study

#### **Contributions to Science** 

#### 1. **Health Literacy, Cognitive Function, and Functional Health Status among Middle-Aged and Older Adults.** 

Our longitudinal study of older adults (R01AG030611) has found evidence to strongly support that health literacy declines over time and is linked to negative health outcomes, including poorer functional health, higher rates of hospitalization, and increased overall mortality (Curtis et al., 2021). Findings from this study strongly suggest that limited health literacy primarily reflects individual differences in a wide range of cognitive skills. We are continuing to follow this cohort to explore strategies that can reduce the cognitive demands placed on older adults managing multiple chronic conditions. In addition, we have begun to investigate these relationships in a younger population (R01AG070212). 

#### 2. **Driving Advances in Chronic Disease Self-Management.** 

Our research team has worked to characterize risk factors of chronic disease self-management in several cognitive aging cohort studies. We have highlighted the complex and varied experiences of older adults living with multiple chronic conditions (MCC), shedding light on the numerous challenges they encounter in managing their health (Bailey et al., 2020). This work has emphasized the critical interplay between cognitive function, psychosocial factors, and health literacy in shaping selfmanagement behaviors and outcomes (Lovett et al., 2023). By better understanding these dynamics, we aim to inform interventions that support older adults in effectively navigating the demands of MCC and improving their quality of life. 

#### 3. **Epigenetic Biomarkers, Biological Age Acceleration, and their Associations with Psychosocial Determinants of Health.** 

My research has focused extensively on understanding how psychosocial and socioeconomic factors influence biological aging and chronic disease risk through epigenetic mechanisms. Using data from large, diverse cohort studies such as the Health and Retirement Study (HRS) and the Multi-Ethnic Study of Atherosclerosis (MESA), I have applied epigenome-wide association studies, mediation modeling, and epigenetic clock analyses to investigate how stress, loneliness, depressive symptoms, and socioeconomic status become biologically embedded. Findings from this research have identified DNA methylation signatures associated with cumulative psychosocial stress and demonstrated that health behaviors mediate a portion of these associations, though a substantial proportion is likely driven by other biological pathways (Opsasnick et al., 2025). Furthermore, I contributed to the development of methylation-based biomarkers of childhood and adult socioeconomic status that predict agerelated chronic conditions and mortality (Schmitz et al., 2025). 

#### 4. **Longitudinal Data Analysis using Trajectory Modeling.** 

Drawing on my experience with observational cohort studies, I have applied longitudinal data analysis techniques, including trajectory modeling, to examine patterns of change in cognitive function, health literacy, and health outcomes over time. This approach has enabled the identification of distinct subgroups with varying trajectories, providing insights into risk factors and protective mechanisms that influence aging and chronic disease management. My work has contributed to a deeper understanding of how individual differences unfold across the lifespan and how they impact long-term health.

### **BUDGET JUSTIFICATION Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai (ISMMS)** 

The proposed project is budgeted for a total of 5 years. Below is a detailed justification for each budget element. 

### **<u>KEY-PERSONNEL</u>** 

**Pablo Knobel, PhD:** Principal Investigator (4.8 calendar months, Years 1–5). Dr. Knobel is a geospatial epidemiologist who bridges exposure science and environmental epidemiology. He is an Instructor in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai and a member of the Institute for Exposomic Research. He has extensive experience in urban exposome and urban health research, including the development and application of high spatiotemporal resolution exposure estimates, the use of electronic health records, and the integration of co-occurring exposures. He has published research on aging and older adult populations, including work with PhenoAge Acceleration and the VA Frailty Index, and has extensive experience working with large retrospective EHR-based cohorts, including the Mount Sinai Health System EHR. 

_Role on project:_ Dr. Knobel will be responsible for the overall coordination and supervision of all study aspects, including hiring, training, and supervising staff and students. He will oversee all statistical analyses, guide a postdoctoral research fellow through the design and analysis phases of the epidemiologic research, and lead all dissemination and science communication efforts. Dr. Knobel will meet weekly with all lab members involved in the project, monthly with Drs. Yitshak Sade, Kloog, and Midya, quarterly with the Northwestern team, and annually with the full study team. Additional meetings will be scheduled as the project demands. 

**Maayan Yitshak Sade,** PhD: Co-Investigator (1.2 calendar months, Years 1–5). Dr. Yitshak Sade is an epidemiologist with a primary research focus in environmental health. She is an Associate Professor in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai and a member of the Institute for Exposomic Research. She has extensive experience in exposure mixture analyses, large-scale data analyses using national cohorts, and longitudinal investigation of how air pollution and the urban environment affect human health. She has published research on aging and older adult populations, with particular focus on cardiometabolic outcomes, and has extensive experience working with large national and retrospective cohorts, including the World Trade Center Health Registry, Medicare, and the Veterans Affairs cohort. Drs. Yitshak Sade and Knobel are co-MPIs on two pilot projects supporting this proposal and have coauthored over 10 publications together. 

_Role on project:_ Dr. Yitshak Sade will oversee the epidemiological and longitudinal aspects of the project across both cohorts, contributing expertise in mixture analyses for Aims 1 and 2 and in the interpretation of longitudinal findings. She will meet monthly with Dr. Knobel and participate in annual full-team meetings. 

**Itai Kloog,** PhD: Co-Investigator (1.2 calendar months, Years 1–5). Dr. Kloog is a health geographer and Professor in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai and a member of the Institute for Exposomic Research. He has expertise in creating, validating, and managing largescale exposure maps, and in integrating and harmonizing multiple geospatial data sources. Dr. Kloog has served as the geospatial lead on all three pilot projects supporting this proposal and has co-authored over 10 publications with Dr. Knobel. 

_Role on project:_ Dr. Kloog will lead the geospatial components of the project, including the integration of already-existing exposure datasets and, when needed, the replication of exposures across cities. He will meet monthly with Dr. Knobel and participate in annual full-team meetings. 

**Vishal Midya,** PhD: Co-Investigator (1.2 calendar months, Years 1–5). Dr. Midya is an Assistant Professor in the Department of Environmental Medicine and Public Health at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, with expertise in mixture methods and interpretable machine learning for environmental health research. He has served as the biostatistical lead on one of the pilot projects supporting this proposal and has provided biostatistical support to Dr. Knobel's research program for the past five years through the biostatistics division of the Department of Environmental Medicine. 

_Role on project:_ Dr. Midya will work closely with Dr. Knobel to ensure that analyses are grounded in biological and epidemiological theory. He will contribute mixture methods expertise in Aims 1 and 2 and lead the machine 

Page 79 

Budget Justification Attachment 



<!-- page 81 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

learning components of the prediction framework in Aim 3. He will co-lead manuscript preparation across all aims. He will meet monthly with Dr. Knobel and participate in annual full-team meetings. 

**Alex Federman, MD** : Co-Investigator (0.6 calendar months, Years 1–5). Dr. Federman is a primary care physician and clinical scientist, Professor of Medicine in the Departments of Medicine and Geriatrics and Palliative Medicine, Director for Research in the Division of General Internal Medicine, and Program Director for the Disparities in Aging and Dementia (DiAD) T32 post-doctoral research training program. His research focuses on issues that complicate healthcare and health outcomes for older adults with complex chronic illnesses. 

_Role on project:_ Dr. Federman will advise on clinical aspects of the project, as well as on policy and practice implications of the obtained results. 

### **<u>NON-KEY PERSONNEL</u>** 

**Postdoctoral Fellow (TBD)** : (12 calendar months, Years 1–5). Funds are requested to support a postdoctoral fellow to work with Dr. Knobel and the team of co-investigators. Dr. Knobel has extensive experience mentoring trainees, ensuring high-quality mentorship and career development for the selected candidate. The selected candidate will hold a PhD in environmental epidemiology or a related field. The postdoctoral fellow will contribute to data analysis and manuscript preparation across all three aims. 

**<mark>Research Assistant (TBD):</mark>** <mark>(6 calendar months, Years 1–5). Funds are requested to support a research assistant to work with Dr. Knobel and provide support to the Postdoctoral Fellow. The analyst will provide research and administrative support across all three aims, including data management, data sharing coordination, and IRB-related activities, ensuring the integrity and organization of study datasets throughout</mark> the project. 

**<mark>Geospatial Technician (TBD ):</mark>** <mark>(6 calendar months, Years 1–5). Funds are requested to support a geospatial technician to provide support for the construction, management, and harmonization of the geospatial exposure database that underpins all three aims. The Technician will maintain reproducible, version-controlled geospatial workflows, perform quality control checks on exposure data, and support the integration of novel exposure</mark> metrics. 

**<mark>FRINGE RATES</mark>** <mark>: Fringe benefit costs are requested at a rate in accordance with the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai negotiated rate of 31% for FY26 forward.</mark> 

### **<u>NON-PERSONNEL COST</u>** 

### **<u>EQUIPMENT</u>** 

**High-performance computer ($10,000 in year 1):** Funds are requested for a high-performance computer capable of geospatial analyses with large exposure datasets. The high expense of these systems is primarily driven by the need for large amounts of RAM (768GB-1TB). Other desirable features include 24+ cores, fast “scratch” NVMe SSDs for rapid I/O, large RAID 6 for local storage of data (30+ TB), and an advanced GPU for use with modern machine-learning libraries (e.g. H20 and TensorFlow). Competitive quotes will be obtained from multiple scientific computing vendors such as Microway, System76, and Dell. 

### **<u>TRAVEL</u>** 

### **<u>Domestic Travel: $8,000 in years 1 & 5 and $4,000 in years 2-4</u>** 

### **<u><mark>Foreign Travel: $3,000 in years 1-5</mark></u>** 

**Scientific conferences:** Funds are requested for the PI, trainees, and colleagues to attend and present papers at one or two conferences per year. The dissemination of results to relevant environmental health audiences such as the International Society for Exposure Science, the International Society for Environmental Epidemiology, as well as transdisciplinary venues through the American Meteorological Society (AMS), American Geophysical Union (AGU), and Society for Epidemiologic Research (SER), and to aging health audiences such as Gerontological Society of America (GSA), will increase the impact of this study while keeping the study team abreast with new scientific developments in these areas and facilitating new collaborations. Funds will cover airfare, registration fees, lodging, and per diem. 

Page 80 

Budget Justification Attachment 



<!-- page 82 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

**Project Meetings in Years 1 and 5:** Funds are requested to support in-person full-team meetings at the start and close of the project. The Year 1 meeting will serve as a project kick-off, bringing together the MSSM and Northwestern teams to align on study protocols, data workflows, and analytic plans across all three aims. The Year 5 meeting will serve as a close-out, focused on integrating findings, finalizing manuscripts, and planning next steps. Meeting location will rotate between sites. Costs cover airfare, lodging, and per diem for 3–4 team members traveling to the host site. 

### **<u>PUBLICATIONS</u>** 

**Publishing/Presenting ($7,000 in years 1-5):** Funds are requested to cover costs of publication page charges and other fees such as poster printing, as part of the dissemination of research results. 

### **<u>OTHER EXPENSES</u>** 

**Lingual editing software ($150 in years 1-5):** We will use linguistic editing software to edit manuscripts and generate high-quality publications. 

**Computing and storage ($760 in years 1-5):** Funds are requested to cover computing services and storage at the Mount Sinai high performance cluster. 

**Mount Sinai data update ($9,000 in year 1):** Funds are requested to cover the costs of extracting and accessing the required data from Mount Sinai. These funds will cover the 50 hours we expect this data extraction will require, at an hourly rate of 180$/hour. 

### **<u>DATA MANAGEMENT AND SHARING COST</u>** 

There are no additional cost for Data Management and Sharing. This application is focused on secondary data analysis from existing data but will also deposit some exposure data to a public repository. We will make data, metadata, and code accessible as much as possible, while maintaining confidentiality and data safety. Our plan to publicly share metadata, code, and data requires an analyst to be responsible for all aspects of data management, code preparation, cleaning, and annotation, and preparation of the associated metadata. We will use best practices for data management and maximizing sharing to the extent permissible given NIH guidance and the ethical/legal/technical limitations. 

The postdoctoral fellows and data analyst, under the guidance of the PI, will be responsible for all aspects related to implementation of the data sharing and management plan: curating data, developing supporting documentation, sharing data through repositories, and local data management considerations. 

**<u>Facilities and Administration (F&A):</u>** Currently, the F&A rate at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai is 68% of Modified Total Direct Costs (MTDC).
