### **Budget Justification** 

_The Urban Exposome and Healthy Aging: From Evidence to Intervention Projections Across Two US Cities_ Northwestern University 

### **Personnel** 

### **KEY PERSONNEL** 

**Michael Wolf, PhD MPH, MA (Site Principal Investigator; 1.2 CM Y1-Y5)** is Professor of Medicine in General Internal Medicine, Associate Division Chief of Research for the Division Chief of General Internal Medicine, and Director of the Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA) in Northwestern University’s Feinberg School of Medicine. Dr. Wolf is a health services researcher and cognitive-behavioral scientist with expertise in adult learning and cognition. For the past 20 years, he has studied health literacy-related issues, spanning the extent and association of limited literacy with various health outcomes, its meaning and measurement, and the development and testing of practical and scalable interventions to improve health communication, behavior, and subsequent health outcomes, its meaning and measurement, and the development and testing of interventions to reduce literacy’s impact on health. He has led in the publication of health literacy-related articles (250+) and has served on multiple national panels on health literacy, health disparities, and chronic disease management – including the proper use of and adherence to multi-drug regimens. His work in health literacy, and also in patient safety, has led to numerous national awards, including the first Pfizer Health Literacy Scholar Award (2002), three Pfizer health literacy visiting professorships (Univ. of Illinois, NYU, Lincoln Memorial), the Institute for Healthcare Advancement Outstanding Health Literacy Research Award, the National Patient Safety Foundation Health Literacy Award, and J. William Fulbright Distinguished Faculty Scholar Award (United Kingdom). 

In 2004, he founded the Health Literacy and Learning Program (HeLP) to create a network of multidisciplinary faculty that could partner together to generate applied, innovative research that would help transform the delivery of healthcare for vulnerable patients (older adults a specific target). Since its inception, HeLP has received >$45 million in NIH, AHRQ, private foundation and industry funding. In 2019, Dr. Wolf was able to build out HeLP and five additional programs in his role as Director of CAHRA. CAHRA seeks to promote informed decision making and actions leading to optimal health and well-being among individuals and families over the life span. 

**_Role on project_ :** Dr. Wolf will be site Principal Investigator **,** and he will provide cognitive science expertise throughout the project as well as experience conducting longitudinal cohort studies examining cognitive function and health outcomes (‘LitCog’; R01AG030611 and ‘MidCog’ R01AG070212). He will contribute to the development and refinement of the study protocols and provide guidance on issues relating to the measurement of major constructs. Along with Dr. Opsasnick and Ms. Yee he will contribute to data management and data analysis for the study. Throughout the project, he will attend regular team meetings to discuss study progress and preliminary findings. In year 5, he will contribute to the interpretation of data and will provide support to disseminate the findings, collaborating with Dr. Knobel and other manuscript writers for this study. 

Dr. Wolf's institutional base salary exceeds the DHHS salary cap, and the amounts requested for salary and fringe benefits in this budget have been calculated to reflect the DHHS salary cap. 

**Lauren Opsasnick, PhD, MS (Co-Investigator; 1.4 CM Y1, 1.39 CM Y2-Y3, 1.38 CM Y4, 1.38 CM Y5)** is an <mark>Assistant Professor</mark> of <mark>Medicine currently employed</mark> in <mark>the Division</mark> of <mark>General Internal Medicine and</mark> an <mark>active member</mark> of <mark>the CAHRA Data Analysis Unit.</mark> In <mark>2024, Dr. Opsasnick completed her PhD</mark> in <mark>Epidemiology Science from the University</mark> of <mark>Michigan School</mark> of <mark>Public health. She has multiple years</mark> of <mark>experience managing and</mark> 

Page 98 

Budget Justification Attachment 



<!-- page 100 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

<mark>analyzing epidemiological data. Currently, Dr. Opsasnick manages the LitCog and MidCog datasets</mark> in <mark>collaboration with Ms. Yeh and</mark> is <mark>familiar with</mark> all <mark>aspects related</mark> to <mark>the data.</mark> 

**_Role on project:_** <mark>Dr. Opsasnick will work closely with Drs. Knobel and Wolf, and Ms. Yeh</mark> to <mark>implement the proposed statistical procedures providing data sets</mark> as <mark>appropriate from the LitCog study</mark> to <mark>the Mt. Sinai study team. Additionally, she will oversee Ms. Yeh</mark> in <mark>controlling the data management and sharing the data with Mt. Sinai. Throughout the project, she will attend regular team meetings</mark> to <mark>discuss study progress and preliminary findings.</mark> 

### **NON-KEY PERSONNEL** 

**Morgan Bonham, BS (Project Manager; 2.40 CM Y1-Y5)** is a Clinical Research Associate and has been involved in Research Project Management in the Division of General Internal Medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine for Dr. Wolf for the last 6 years. She manages the NIA-funded LitCog and MidCog studies. Prior to becoming project manager, she also worked as a Research Study Coordinator for 4 years. Ms. Bonham is extremely knowledgeable about research and project management, including Federal and IRB regulations for human subjects research, as well as participant recruitment and retention. 

**_Role on project:_** Under the guidance of Dr. Wolf, Ms. Bonham’s responsibilities will fall into two broad categories: 1) Research Management: tasks within the category include maintenance of IRB records and ensuring the project is in compliance with Federal and IRB regulations for human subjects research;  3) Data Management and Sharing: She will maintain an electronic database, and confirm data is being sent to collaborating sites in a secure and timely manner. 

**Fangyu Yeh, MS (Statistical Analyst; 2.40 CM Y1-Y5)** is currently employed in the Division of General Internal Medicine and serves as a statistical analyst in CAHRA. She has over 5 years of experience managing and analyzing epidemiological data. Ms. Yeh currently manages the several of Dr. Wolf’s aging datasets and is familiar with all aspects related to the data. 

**_Role on project:_** Ms. Yeh will assist with statistical procedures by cleaning and prepping data and will be directly supervised by Dr. Opsasnick to run any basic statistical analyses needed. She will support Dr. Opsasnick with the data management and sharing requests. 

Base salaries are projected with 3% inflation each year, but never exceed the DHHS salary cap.

**SPECIFIC AIMS:** The world's population is aging rapidly, and quality of life is as important as longevity. Yet for many, a longer life brings more years in poor health<sup>1</sup> , and the global burden of ill-health and medical expenditure is concentrated in later life<sup>2</sup> . Given the complexity of the aging process, no single and reliable measure can capture how healthily someone is aging<sup>3</sup> . The **Healthy Aging Phenotype (HAP)**<sup>3</sup> addresses this by organizing healthy aging across five domains: physiological and metabolic health, physical capability, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing, together encapsulating the ability to remain socially engaged, productive, and functionally independent<sup>3</sup> . The exposome, or the totality of environmental exposures over the life course<sup>4</sup> , enables an accurate characterization of how the environment shapes health by considering cooccurring exposures across time. The **Urban Exposome** is a facet of the exposome characterized by two key features: its exposures are geospatial and, in most cases, directly modifiable by decision-makers. It encompasses three dimensions: built (e.g., greenspace, walkability, food environment, land use), social (e.g., neighborhood perceptions, crime), and ambient (e.g., fine particulate matter mass and composition, temperature, noise). Heterogeneity in HAP is not random, as many urban exposures have been found to promote or impede it<sup>5</sup> : for example, greenness<sup>6</sup> , walkability<sup>7</sup> , safety<sup>8,9</sup> , lifestyle patterns<sup>10</sup> , socioeconomic status<sup>11</sup> , air pollution, noise, and heat<sup>12-14</sup> have been linked to different aspects of HAP. 

However, three gaps remain. **(1)** while the urban exposome has been linked to birth weight<sup>15</sup> , obesity<sup>16</sup> , and type 2 diabetes<sup>17</sup> , no study has linked the urban exposome to healthy aging: leaving the cumulative and joint effects of co-occurring urban exposures uncharacterized. **(2)** most existing work focuses on single aging outcomes: missing the multidimensional nature of healthy aging across biological, physical, cognitive, psychological, and social domains. **(3)** Evidence rarely extends beyond association to the actionable question of what would happen to aging outcomes if specific urban features were improved: leaving decision-makers without the evidence base needed to prioritize investments in healthy urban aging. 

This proposal bridges the HAP and Urban Exposome frameworks by investigating the concurrent associations of a wide array of urban environmental exposures with all HAP aspects. HAP domains are measured with validated metrics across the two cohorts: the Mount Sinai Health System’s (MSHS) Electronic Health Records (EHR) provides EHR-derived physiological and metabolic aging indicators, while the Health Literacy and Cognitive Function Among Older Adults (LitCog) cohort provides administered assessments of physical function, cognition, depression, and social wellbeing. Neither cohort alone can capture the full range of aging outcomes this proposal addresses. 

**Aim 1: Assess associations between the Urban Exposome and physiological and metabolic aging indicators.** Using ~20,000 patients (~160,000 person-years) from MSHS EHR, we will derive two complementary aging indicators: Phenotypic Age Acceleration (PhenoAgeAccel)<sup>18</sup> , a validated biological age measure based on clinical biomarkers, and the Veterans Affairs Frailty Index (VA-FI)<sup>19</sup> , a validated, 31-item EHRbased frailty measure<sup>19</sup> . We will estimate the joint mixture association and the relative contribution of each exposure using quantile G-computation (qGcomp)<sup>20</sup> . 

**Aim 2: Assess associations between the Urban Exposome and physical capability, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing aging metrics.** Using 900 Chicago participants with up to 15 years of follow-up from LitCog, we will examine how the Urban Exposome shapes aging measured through standardized questionnaires. We will simultaneously estimate associations between the urban exposome and both the baseline level and rate of change of physical function (measure through PROMIS), cognition (MMSE), depression (PROMIS), and social support (Lubben Social Network Scale). We will apply Bayesian Varying Coefficient Kernel Machine Regression (BVCKMR)<sup>21</sup> which accommodates nonlinear exposure-response relationships and exposure interactions. 

**Aim 3: Explore the projected population health impact of feasible urban environmental interventions on healthy aging.** Using machine learning prediction we will project healthy aging outcomes under scenarios in which specific urban exposures are modified to levels already observed in comparable neighborhoods. **Aim 3.a:** We will train ensemble prediction models (elastic net, random forest, and XGBoost) on each cohort's linked exposure-outcome data. **Aim 3.b:** We will define grounded intervention scenarios (such as expanding greenspace or improving the food environment) **Aim 3.c:** We will apply the trained models to synthetic populations with modified scenario-based exposures and compare projected outcomes to a business-as-usual baseline, generating quantified estimates of population health benefit. 

**Impact:** As urban populations age rapidly, cities face mounting pressure to invest wisely in environments that support healthy aging. This proposal is innovative in being the first to characterize the urban exposome in relation to comprehensive healthy aging metrics. Its <u>significance</u> lies in generating actionable evidence that urban decision-makers can leverage.

### **A. IMPORTANCE OF RESEARCH** 

### **A.1. Significance** 

**Healthy aging is in the spotlight of public health research and policy-making.** The global ratio of individuals aged 65 and older to those under 65 is projected to rise from 1:11 to 1:6 by 2050: from 703 million to 1.5 billion<sup>1</sup> . The majority of this growth will occur in urban areas, where environmental conditions are shaped by humanmade infrastructure and policy decisions<sup>1</sup> . As the older adult population grows, it will increasingly shape the burden placed on healthcare systems, caregiving networks, and social infrastructure<sup>2</sup> . _This proposal generates the epidemiological evidence and scenario-based projections that urban decision-makers need to act on the environment as a lever for healthy aging._ 

**Healthy aging is a multidimensional process that no single measure can fully capture**<sup>**3**</sup> **.** Greater life expectancy does not guarantee more years of healthy aging<sup>4</sup> . Aging decline is not uniform: some older adults experience steep declines in health and function, while others maintain vitality and capacity well into late life<sup>5</sup> . The Healthy Aging Phenotype (HAP) framework organizes healthy aging across five domains (physiological and metabolic health, physical capability, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing) capturing what it means to stay functionally independent, socially connected, and productive as we age<sup>6</sup> . Each domain offers a different and non-redundant lens on the same underlying aging process. Measuring this heterogeneity requires fundamentally different data across domains: some indicators depend on clinical biomarkers and administrative records, while others rely on questionnaire-based assessments or clinician-performed assessments. This distinction has constrained any single study's ability to capture healthy aging in full. _Recognizing that no single metric captures the aging process, we will measure healthy aging across six outcomes anchored in HAP._ 

**Aging heterogeneity is not random: the environments people live in shape how they age**<sup>**7-9**</sup> . For example, greenness<sup>10</sup> and walkability<sup>11</sup> are linked to better mental health, cognitive function, well-being, and physical capability among older adults. Environmental biodiversity is also associated with aspects of human health<sup>12</sup> . Food environments are associated with malnutrition and frailty<sup>13</sup> .  Lifestyle patterns, including diet and physical activity, are influenced by the built and social environments and simultaneously influence healthy aging<sup>14</sup> . The sociodemographic composition of the neighborhood of residence may also be associated with increased odds of frailty<sup>7</sup> , and negative neighborhood perception is associated with reduced physical activity and increased sedentary time<sup>15</sup> . Safety is crucial for seniors’ well-being<sup>16</sup> , with associations between self-reported safety and self-reported health<sup>17</sup> . Furthermore, socioeconomic status is linked to differences in aging-related outcomes and life expectancy<sup>18</sup> . Air pollution, noise, and heat<sup>19-21</sup> are linked to worse mental health, cognitive function, wellbeing, and physical capability among older adults. Prior studies have examined combinations of exposures: for example, the joint associations of air pollution, greenness, and noise with mortality<sup>22</sup> and stroke<sup>23</sup> . However, comprehensive characterizations remain rare, particularly in aging populations. _Aims 1 and 2 provide the first comprehensive test of how the full urban environment jointly shapes healthy aging._ 

**Environmental epidemiology tends to focus on single exposures, but life exposes us to many factors simultaneously.** Understanding the links between the environment and healthy aging requires a framework that captures this complexity. The <u>exposome</u> — the totality of environmental exposures, both chemical and nonchemical, encountered throughout life<sup>24</sup> — offers this framework by considering co-occurring exposures across time. Because concurrent exposures shape the influence of each individual exposure on health, single-exposure approaches may under- or overestimate effects by missing additive, amplifying, confounding, or synergistic relationships<sup>25</sup> . Single-exposure models remain a necessary first step: they establish comparability with existing literature, define baseline associations, and help interpret more complex mixture analyses. But multi-exposure models are an essential next step to account for the reality that no exposure acts in isolation. The <u>Urban Exposome operationalizes this framework for the city environment. From a geospatial perspective, the Urban</u> Exposome can be conceptualized as the interaction among three core domains — built, social, and ambient — that together characterize the environmental conditions under which most older adults live, and that urban decision-makers can, in many cases, directly modify. Built exposures refer to the human-made physical features of the environment; social exposures encompass the demographic, socioeconomic, and cultural characteristics of communities; and ambient exposures include external environmental conditions. The Urban Exposome has been linked to multiple health outcomes, including birth weight<sup>26</sup> , obesity<sup>27</sup> , and type 2 diabetes<sup>28</sup> . However, no study has extended this framework to healthy aging outcomes. _We treat the Urban Exposome as an operational framework: it defines which exposures we measure, how we model them jointly, and how we interpret the results._ 

**Urban environments shape aging through multiple converging biological and behavioral pathways.** The three exposure domains act through distinct but interconnected mechanisms. Built environment exposures act 

Page 106 

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 108 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

primarily through behavioral intermediaries: walkable, green neighborhoods promote habitual physical activity and limit sedentary time, key determinants of functional capacity, cognitive reserve, and metabolic health<sup>10,11,14</sup> . Access to healthy food environments reduces the risk of malnutrition and obesity, both established drivers of frailty<sup>13</sup> . Social environment exposures, including neighborhood deprivation, crime, and perceived unsafety, sustain chronic psychological stress, elevating allostatic load and suppressing engagement in health-promoting behaviors<sup>7,15</sup> . Ambient exposures, particularly fine particulate matter, noise, and heat, drive systemic inflammation, oxidative stress, and autonomic nervous system dysregulation<sup>19-21</sup> . These pathways do not operate independently: urban residents experience ambient, built, and social exposures simultaneously, and their joint effect on aging likely exceeds any single-pathway account<sup>33,34,36</sup> . _This proposal tests the joint associations of all three domains on six HAP-related outcomes, each measuring a different downstream consequence of these pathways._ 

**Beyond disease prevention: examining how environments shape aging over time.** Most environmental epidemiology research has focused on identifying risk factors for disease onset. There is an equally urgent need to identify modifiable factors that contribute to worse outcomes among individuals already experiencing agerelated decline, with the goal of slowing deterioration and preserving functional independence<sup>29</sup> . _In this proposal, we leverage two cohorts with longitudinal follow-up and measure healthy aging through continuous outcomes to track environmental influences on the full arc of aging, from early subclinical change through advanced decline._ 

**Environmental epidemiology has historically focused on characterizing associations, leaving a translational gap between evidence and actionable inference**<sup>**30**</sup> **.** This is consequential given that many geospatial exposures are, by nature, modifiable through urban policy and planning, creating a direct pathway from epidemiological evidence to actionable estimates of healthy aging benefit. Urban decision-makers require not only evidence that specific exposures are associated with healthy aging, but quantitative estimates of the population health gains achievable under feasible interventions. Scenario analysis offers one such pathway, enabling researchers to compare observed health burdens against those projected under hypothetical exposure modifications<sup>31</sup> . Complementarily, predictive modeling approaches offer a promising avenue to further bridge this gap<sup>32</sup> . In the context of scenario and intervention design, this convergence is particularly valuable: machine learning based predictive models excel at handling the collinearity, complex interactions, and temporality inherent to urban exposome data, and when the scientific question concerns pragmatic, population-level health changes under realistic interventions rather than the precise magnitude of a single exposure's effect, their application is a deliberate analytical choice. _Aim 3 moves beyond association, quantifying projections of population health benefit under feasible urban interventions._ 

### **A.2. Innovation** 

This proposal offers four innovations that together distinguish it from all prior work on urban environments and healthy aging. 

**First, this proposal is the first to characterize the full urban exposome in relation to healthy aging.** Prior multi-exposure studies in aging typically incorporate two to three exposures and rarely cross domain boundaries. Systematic, cross-domain characterization within a unified analytical framework is absent from the aging literature. 

**Second, this proposal measures healthy aging as the multidimensional process it is, across all HAP domains.** Prior environmental epidemiology studies have examined HAP domains in isolation or at most two domains together. Integrating six different outcomes within a unified framework allows us to ask whether the urban exposome shapes healthy aging broadly or selectively, a question single-outcome studies cannot answer. 

**Third, two complementary cohorts and matched statistical methods provide the strongest possible evidence for each outcome.** The MSHS EHR (~20,000 patients, ~160,000 person-years) supplies the data depth for EHR-derived aging indicators. LitCog (900 Chicago participants, up to 15 years of follow-up) supplies the administered assessments needed for functional, cognitive, psychological, and social aging metrics. Neither cohort alone could generate the breadth and depth of evidence this design produces together. 

**Fourth, our scenario-based projection framework is internally consistent with the association evidence this proposal generates.** Prior simulation studies apply exposure-response functions borrowed from the literature to separate populations, introducing inconsistency between the evidence base and the projection target. We project through prediction models trained on the same cohorts used for Aims 1 and 2, which capture the full multivariate exposure structure simultaneously and remain grounded in the same populations, exposures, and outcome measures. All scenario targets are additionally constrained to levels already observed in comparable neighborhoods, ensuring projections reflect what urban environments have demonstrably delivered rather than theoretical optima with no empirical basis.

### **A.3. Expected Impact:** 

This project will generate the most comprehensive urban exposome evidence base for healthy aging to date, revealing which dimensions of urban exposures matter for which dimensions of aging. Beyond association, it will produce grounded scenario-based projections that translate epidemiological findings into quantified estimates of population health benefit under feasible urban interventions. Together, this epidemiological evidence and these scenario-based projections will advance scientific knowledge of how cities shape aging, generate hypotheses for future targeted research, inform clinical practice for older adults living in high-risk urban environments, and give urban decision-makers the evidence they need to invest wisely in environments that support healthy aging. 

### **B. RIGOR AND FEASIBILITY**

### **B.1. Preliminary and published findings:** 

The preliminary work below spans both cohorts, all <u>Exposome Wide Association Study (ExWAS) X</u> three aims, and the full arc from exposure science <u>Quantile G Computation (qGcomp) X</u> to epidemiologic association to scenario-based Bayesian Varying Coefficient Kernel Machine X <u>Regression (BVCKMR)</u> projection. Together, these studies establish that Machine Learnin <mark>g</mark> Ensemble X we have worked with each outcome in its appropriate cohort and applied the statistical approaches described above, that the urban exposome shapes aging outcomes across HAP domains, and that feasible urban interventions can produce measurable population health benefits. 

**Multidimensional urban nature exposure is associated with older adults’ health**<sup>**33**</sup> **.** Among 36,830 NYC residents aged ≥55 years, we classified surrounding urban nature into five residential exposure profiles using eight high-resolution indicators within 300-meter buffers. Over a median follow-up of 3.8 years, compared to Low Nature, Waterfront (HR = 0.88; 95% CI: 0.81–0.95), Park Access (HR = 0.89; 95% CI: 0.84–0.94), and Street Trees (HR = 0.92; 95% CI: 0.87–0.98) profiles were each associated with lower incident ASCVD risk compared to the Low Green profile. 

**Social environment and phenotypic age acceleration**<sup>**34**</sup> **.** Using the MSHS EHR, we examined associations between the social environment and PhenoAgeAccel among older adults in New York City. We applied quantile g-computation. Higher social vulnerability was associated with greater PhenoAgeAccel: a decile increase across all four social vulnerability dimensions corresponded to a 0.23-year gap between biological and chronological age. The socioeconomic status dimension drove the association, accounting for 61% of the mixture weight. 

**PM2.5 source and composition shapes cardiovascular mortality risk amongst older adults**<sup>**35**</sup> **.** In a national mixture analysis of ~66 million Medicare enrollees (2000–2016), we found higher ASCVD mortality risk associated with PM2.5 from oil combustion, industrial, coal and biomass burning, and motor vehicle sources, with the largest effect observed for coal and biomass burning (RR 1.065; 95% CI 1.062–1.067 per IQR increase). 

**PM2.5 and its chemical components and frailty velocity.** In preliminary analysis among 20,880 MSHS patients (2011–2019) we screened PM2.5 and 15 PM2.5 components against frailty velocity (an approach analogous to an ExWAS). Higher PM2.5 exposure was associated with accelerated frailty velocity (β = 0.013; 95% CI: 0.006, 0.022 per IQR increase). In component-specific models adjusted for the PM2.5 residual, 12 of 15 components were associated with frailty acceleration, with the largest effects observed for lead (β = 0.103; 95% CI: 0.057, 0.149). 

**Long-term associations between urban exposures and distinct aging outcomes in the LitCog cohort.** In preliminary analyses among 900 older adults followed over 15 years (2008–2023), we used linear mixed models to evaluate associations of PM2.5 and NDVI with physical function, cognition, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing. Higher PM2.5 exposure was associated with lower MMSE scores (β = −0.23; 95% CI: −0.34, −0.13) and worse physical function (β = −0.52; 95% CI: −0.92, −0.13). Greater NDVI was associated with fewer

depressive symptoms (β = −5.05; 95% CI: −9.51, −0.59) and higher social engagement (β = 4.06; 95% CI: 0.95, 7.16). 

**Co-occurring urban exposures with frailty and depression**<sup>**36**</sup> **.** In a preliminary analysis among 18,861 general responders of the WTC Health Program, we evaluated associations of PM2.5, temperature, and Green View Index (GVI) exposures with frailty and depression. We found elevated frailty risk associated with higher PM2.5 exposure (2.16%; 95% CI 0.51, 3.83) and hotter temperatures (0.91%; 95% CI 0.54, 1.27), while higher GVI was associated with lower frailty risk (−1.51%; 95% CI −2.28%, −0.74%).

### **B.2. Exposure Assessment and Harmonization** 

**Exposure selection:** We will build a geospatial database covering ~40 diverse built, social, and ambient exposures (Table 2). We selected these exposures based on their hypothesized relevance to aging, drawing on established literature and conceptual frameworks in environmental epidemiology<sup>27-29</sup> . Many have documented links to health outcomes; others are relatively novel in the context of aging research. Including both well-studied and novel exposures reflects reflects a core goal of the exposome approach: to identify environmental determinants of health that prior, single-exposure studies would have missed. By including both well-studied and understudied exposures, we balance discovery with comparability to existing evidence.

|Urban<br>Green<br>Space<br>Quality<br>Dimensions|Park quality index derived from convolutional neural networks applied to street view and satellite imagery. Nine<br>distinct quality dimensions.|

**Repository exposures:** We will draw on harmonized geospatial and environmental data already assembled on the Mount Sinai GREENER platform (UL1TR001433), supported by the Clinical and Translational Science Awards (CTSA) program (NCATS, NIH), a geospatial repository that aggregates commonly used environmental datasets, including spatial linkages. This approach offers three advantages: it substantially improves feasibility by leveraging community-vetted, standardized resources; it enables comparability across cohorts and studies by using harmonized exposure metrics; and it supports reproducible, automated workflows that reduce manual processing and increase transparency. Dr. Kloog co-leads the GREENER platform. 

**Team-developed exposures:** Repository datasets provide broad coverage but cannot capture every dimension of the urban exposome: particularly city-specific conditions and exposures too novel to have been widely operationalized. We will therefore complement repository data with exposure metrics developed by the research team. These will include the geoprocessing of urban nature metrics (Figure 2) and crime rates, as well as novel exposures developed through complementary pilot projects. We have developed an urban green space quality metric through a pilot grant from the Gateway Exposome Coordinating Center (NIA) (1U24AG088894). We have also developed bird biodiversity metrics through a pilot grant from the Mount Sinai Center on Health and Environment Across the LifeSpan (HEALS) (P30ES023515) . We draw entirely on existing exposure datasets; no new exposure data or generation is required. The one exception is replication of existing metrics across cities: most exposure datasets are already available for both New York City and Chicago, but where a metric has been derived for only one city, we will extend it to the other provided the required raw data are available.

**Harmonization.** All exposures will be harmonized to the census tract and year, creating a consistent spatiotemporal unit of analysis across both cohorts. We will apply multiple imputation by chained equations (MICE) to recover missing exposures rather than exclude them, preserving sample size and reducing the risk of bias from non-random missingness. We will do sensitivity analysis without exposure imputation for all analysis. 

**Exposure assignment.** Both cohorts include detailed residential address histories. We will geocode participant addresses into geographic coordinates, validate the geocoding, and link them to census tracts. We will then assign exposures annually based on each participant's residence in that year, capturing changes in both residential location and environmental conditions over follow-up. 

### **B.3. Approach for Aim 1** 

Physiological and metabolic health is the HAP domain most accessible through clinical records, and the MSHS EHR gives us the scale to test the full urban exposome against it. These findings will enable comparability with the existing single-exposure literature and deepen our understanding of how the urban exposome shapes physiological aging.

We will leverage 14 years of EHR data from MSHS, encompassing seven hospitals, >400 ambulatory practice locations, and >7,200 physicians. The patient population is diverse, including all ages, backgrounds, and healthcare needs. <u>Inclusion criteria:</u> The study population will include individuals ≥55 years old who had an MSHS primary care physician in 2011–2025. Sample: The estimated sample size based on previous publications with the same dataset and other aging-related outcomes is ~20.000 (Table 3). Follow-up: People will be entered into the cohort on the first year in which they have any documented encounter within MSHS and followed for each calendar year until the first recorded outcome, death, or censoring. If two consecutive years show no indication of MSHS usage, the last year of service usage indicated will be considered the last year of follow-up. Our previous analysis in this cohort shows that missing data and errors due to low EHR data continuity are minimal with this approach<sup>37</sup> . 

### **B.3.2. Outcomes** 

**Phenotypic Age Acceleration (PhenoAgeAccel):** Biological age is defined as: “biological parameters of an organism that either alone or in some multivariate composite will, in the absence of disease, better predict functional capability at some late age than chronological age”<sup>38</sup> . PhenoAge is a composite score including chronological age and nine different clinical laboratory measures: Albumin, Creatinine, Glucose, C-reactive protein, Lymphocyte percent, Mean cell volume, Red cell distribution width, Alkaline phosphatase, and White blood cell count<sup>39</sup> . <u>PhenoAgeAccel measures the degree to which a person is younger or older than their chronological age, with positive values indicating that the biological age is older than the chronological age,</u> negative values indicating that the biological age is younger than the chronological age, and zero values indicating a perfect match between chronological and biological aging<sup>40</sup> . We will derive PhenoAgeAccel using the formula described by Levine _et al._<sup>39</sup> . 

Laboratory values drawn during inpatient care may reflect acute physiological stress rather than underlying biological aging trajectories. To minimize this source of misclassification, we will exclude biomarker measurements obtained during inpatient encounters, restricting our derivation of PhenoAge to outpatient results. Additionally, six of the nine required biomarkers (creatinine, glucose, lymphocyte percent, mean cell volume, red cell distribution width, and white blood cell count) are part of standard panels drawn routinely at annual wellness visits. The remaining three (albumin, CRP, and alkaline phosphatase) are obtained more selectively, often when a specific clinical concern arises. In a prior analysis using the same MSHS EHR cohort,<sup>34</sup> CRP accounted for ~63% of person-years with incomplete data to calculate PhenoAgeAccel, albumin for ~16%, and alkaline phosphatase for ~9%. By contrast, the common markers were missing in fewer than 1% of person-years each, confirming that missingness is largely driven by the non-routine biomarkers rather than broad data gaps. For person-years with missing values, we will apply multiple imputation by chained equations (MICE). In the previous publication MICE imputation yielded estimates consistent with complete-case analyses while substantially increasing the analyzable sample<sup>34</sup> . 

**Veterans Affairs Frailty Index (VA-FI):** Frailty is prevalent among older adults, resulting from cumulative decline in multiple physiological systems<sup>41</sup> . Frailty is marked by an individual's heightened susceptibility to adverse events, including mortality, morbidity, disability, and hospitalizations<sup>42</sup> . We will utilize the Veterans Affairs Frailty Index (VA-FI)<sup>43</sup> to estimate frailty. VA-FI is a validated model that measures frailty by adding 31 deficits drawn <u>from diagnostic and procedure codes. As standardized procedure with VA-FI, each variable will be assessed</u> annually using a 3-year lookback window (the year of assessment and two prior years). If a relevant claim is present during that window, the corresponding deficit will be coded as present. The VA-FI will be calculated by 

Page 111 

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 113 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

dividing the number of observed deficits by the total number of possible deficits, producing a continuous score from 0 to 1. 

### **B.3.3. Statistical Analysis** 

**Exposome Wide Association Study (ExWAS)** systematically screens each exposure against each health outcome through a series of independent models (one per exposure), providing a comprehensive scan across the full exposure range. The ExWAS complements our qGcomp approach by providing evidence to compare our results with the existing single-exposure literature and a baseline associations. In each model, the exposure of interest is entered as the primary predictor alongside a fixed set of covariates, and the resulting beta coefficient and p-value characterize the association between that exposure and the outcome. For both PhenoAgeAccel and VA-FI, we will use generalized additive models (GAMs) with a random intercept to account for repeated measures within individuals and a penalized spline for calendar year to flexibly capture outcome trajectories over time, treating each exposure as a time-varying predictor and adjusting for individual and area-level confounders. Testing ~40 exposures simultaneously inflates the probability of false-positive findings. Following standard practice in ExWAS, we will apply the Benjamini-Hochberg FDR procedure at a threshold of q < 0.05, which controls the expected proportion of false discoveries among all rejected null hypotheses<sup>44</sup> . Because exposome components are often correlated with one another<sup>45</sup> , we will also consider dependency-aware FDR extensions such as the Benjamini-Yekutieli approach that accounts for correlations between tests, providing a more accurate estimate of false discoveries when the independence assumption does not hold<sup>46</sup> . 

**Quantile G Computation (qGcomp)** complements ExWAS by estimating the joint effect of the full exposure mixture. qGcomp estimates the association of simultaneously shifting all exposome components by one quantile<sup>47</sup> . We will report the overall mixture effect, direction-specific estimates, and individual exposure weights with directionality, identifying which exposures contribute most and whether their influence is protective or harmful. We will apply the mixed-effects extension of qGcomp with a random intercept for repeated measures and a spline for calendar year, consistent with the ExWAS specification, treating exposures as time-varying predictors and adjusting for individual and area-level confounders.  qGcomp is well-suited to the structure of this cohort for two reasons. First, qGcomp allows individual exposures to contribute in opposing directions simultaneously. Second, its mixed-effects extension accommodates time-varying exposures and repeated measures. 

**Confounders:** Models will adjust for age, sex, self-reported race/ethnicity, insurance type (a proxy for socioeconomic status), neighborhood poverty, and neighborhood racial composition, which we selected based on prior literature and DAG-informed variable selection. We deliberately exclude chronic conditions and other health metrics such as BMI, as these sit on the causal pathway. 

### **B.3.4. Limitations and alternative approaches.** 

**(1)** The VA-FI was originally developed and validated in Veterans Affairs EHR data, which may differ from commercial health system records in coding conventions, encounter structures, and clinical workflows. Differences in how diagnostic and procedure codes are recorded could affect deficit ascertainment and index validity. To assess feasibility prior to the proposed work, we conducted preliminary analyses using the MSHS EHR and confirmed that all 31 VA-FI deficit codes are present and calculable in our dataset, with distributions consistent with published VA-FI values. **(2)** Person-years with low EHR continuity may introduce outcome misclassification: if individuals disengage from care intermittently, absent diagnostic codes may reflect gaps in documentation rather than true deficit absence, biasing VA-FI estimates downward. We address this limitation in two ways. First, our cohort design censors participants after two consecutive years of no MSHS encounters, limiting the extent to which code absence reflects disengagement rather than true deficit absence. Second, older adults tend to maintain stronger and more consistent connections to their healthcare systems than younger populations<sup>48</sup> , and the MSHS primary care population in particular exhibits high EHR continuity (89% of personyears with continuity index >0.6), further mitigating this concern. Additionally, we will restrict to person-years with a continuity index above 0.8 and compare estimates to the primary analysis. **(3)** MICE imputation assumes missingness is at random conditional on observed covariates; if missingness in the three non-routine PhenoAgeAccel biomarkers (CRP, albumin, alkaline phosphatase) is related to the outcome itself, imputed estimates could be biased. We will compare MICE-imputed results to complete-case results and, if estimates differ substantially, report both and discuss the discrepancy. **(4)** If GAM convergence is problematic across the full set of ExWAS models, we will use linear mixed-effects models with restricted cubic splines as an alternative, which provides comparable flexibility for nonlinear exposure-response estimation. **(5)** We will examine whether results are robust to the choice of quantile cutpoints by repeating the mixture analysis using four to ten quantiles. **(6)** If the mixed-effects qGcomp extension encounters instability with the full cross-domain mixture, we will run 

Page 112 

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 114 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

domain-specific models separately for the built, social, and ambient domains: preserving the mixture framework while reducing dimensionality and enabling direct comparison of domain-level effects. 

### **B.4. Approach for Aim 2** 

Where Aim 1 captures biological aging through clinical records, Aim 2 reaches the domains of HAP that only administered assessments can measure: physical capability, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing. Confirming these associations across domains and across both cohorts will establish that the urban exposome's influence on healthy aging is broad rather than selective, and strengthen the evidence base for multi-domain intervention. 

**Hypothesis 2:** _Protective urban exposome features will be associated with better physical function, cognition, and social wellbeing and lower depression, while harmful exposures will be associated with worse outcomes across all four domains._

### **B.4.2. Outcomes:** 

Aim 2 measures healthy aging across four outcomes: Physical capability, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing each capture a distinct and non-redundant dimension of how people age. Each outcome has been prospectively measured in LitCog in six assessment waves spanning 15 years. Together, these outcomes allow us to characterize not just whether the urban exposome is associated with aging trajectories, but across which domains its influence is strongest. Each outcome is assessed with a validated, widely used instrument, enabling comparison with the broader literature (Table 5).

### **B.4.3. Statistical analysis:** 

**Bayesian Varying Coefficient Kernel Machine Regression (BVCKMR):** To estimate associations between the urban exposome and aging trajectories in LitCog, we will apply BVCKMR<sup>49</sup> , a statistical method designed to model the joint effects of multiple exposures on health outcomes measured repeatedly over time. Unlike standard regression approaches and other mixture methods, BVCKMR does not assume a linear relationship between exposures and outcomes. Instead, it flexibly estimates the exposure-response function, capturing nonlinear and 

Page 113 

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 115 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

interactive effects across the full mixture simultaneously. BVCKMR is fit separately for each of the four outcomes (physical function, cognitive function, psychological wellbeing, and social wellbeing) using all available assessment waves (T1 through T6). The outcome at each wave is modeled as a function of the urban exposome measured at the corresponding time point, a smooth function of follow-up time estimated via a penalized spline, a set of individual-level and area-level covariates, and random intercept for repeated measures . A key feature of BVCKMR is that it simultaneously estimates two distinct exposure-response functions: one for the baseline level of the outcome at T1, and one for the rate of change in the outcome across waves. This allows us to investigate whether the urban exposome is associated with where participants start on a given aging trajectory and whether it shapes how quickly that trajectory changes over the course of follow-up. From these estimates, we will derive the relative importance of each urban exposome component for both baseline and trajectory for each outcome, quantified as the difference in the predicted exposure-response function at high versus low exposure levels holding all other exposures at their median. Pairwise interaction plots will further allow us to identify whether specific exposure combinations amplify or dampen each other's effects on aging outcomes. 

### **B.4.4. Limitations and alternative approaches.** 

**(1)** Attrition across LitCog's six waves is substantial (roughly 55% of participants are lost by T4) and if dropout is related to both urban exposures and aging outcomes, trajectory estimates could be biased toward healthier survivors. We will address this by comparing the sociodemographic and baseline outcome characteristics of retained and lost-to-follow-up participants at each wave, and applying inverse probability weighting to reweight the analytic sample toward the baseline population; we will report both weighted and unweighted trajectory estimates. **(2)** The MMSE has a known ceiling effect in cognitively intact older adults, compressing variability at the upper end of the scale and reducing sensitivity to early decline. We will run a sensitivity analysis restricted to participants scoring below 27 at baseline, where the instrument is more sensitive to change. **(3)** BVCKMR is computationally demanding, and convergence is not guaranteed for all four outcomes at LitCog's sample size. If the model fails to converge for any outcome, we will apply quantile G-computation within a linear mixed-effects framework as a fallback: an approach that captures mixture effects and accommodates repeated measures while placing fewer computational demands on the data. **(4)** If the full cross-domain mixture of ~40 exposures proves unstable, we will run domain-specific BVCKMR models separately for the built, social, and ambient domains, consistent with the approach described for Aim 1. 

**Confounders:** Models will adjust for age, sex, self-reported race/ethnicity, education, income, employment status, neighborhood poverty and neighborhood racial composition. As in Aim 1, confounders are selected based on prior literature and DAG-informed variable selection and we do not adjust for chronic conditions. 

### **B.5. Approach for Aim 3** 

Aims 1 and 2 establish which urban exposures matter, how much, and how their effects unfold over time. Aim 3 translates that evidence into a planning-relevant question: _if specific exposures are modified to levels already observed in comparable neighborhoods, how would healthy aging outcomes shift at the population level?_ This kind of scenario-based health projection has an established precedent in environmental epidemiology: health impact assessment studies have projected mortality and disease burden under urban planning scenarios by applying observational exposure-response functions to hypothetical exposure shifts<sup>50,51</sup> . Our approach shares this logic but differs in mechanism: rather than applying exposure-response functions borrowed from the literature to a separate population, we project through prediction models trained on the same cohorts used for projection, which capture the full multivariate exposure structure simultaneously and are internally consistent with the evidence generated in Aims 1 and 2. We frame this aim explicitly as exploratory. Our prediction models learn associations from observational data, not causal structures, and we make no counterfactual identification claims. What we produce is a prioritization tool: data-grounded projections of how HAP outcomes would shift under a specified change in the urban environment, not a substitute for a randomized trial. This approach is consistent with an emerging framework in environmental epidemiology that pairs predictive modeling with scenario analysis to bridge the gap between association evidence and actionable population-level inference<sup>30,32</sup> . **Hypothesis 3:** _Scenario-based projections of feasible urban interventions will show meaningful improvements in healthy aging outcomes compared to a business-as-usual baseline, with combined multi-domain interventions producing larger projected benefits than any single intervention alone._ 

### **B.5.1. Aim 3a: Train an ensemble prediction model on linked exposure-outcome data** 

We will train two separate ensemble prediction models, one for the MSHS EHR cohort and one for LitCog, preserving the integrity of each dataset's temporal structure, outcome measurement, and population characteristics. The prediction task is framed as a time-series, multi-output problem: given a participant's exposome profile, prior outcome values, and sociodemographic covariates at time _t_ , the model simultaneously 

Page 114 

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 116 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

predicts all outcomes at t+1. In MSHS, _t_ is a calendar year; in LitCog, _t_ and _t+1_ correspond to consecutive assessment waves. Joint prediction across outcomes allows the model to exploit correlations, for example, that frailty and biological age acceleration tend to co-occur, rather than treating each outcome as independent. 

The ensemble combines three algorithms: elastic net (regularized linear regression), random forest (variance reduction through averaging of independent trees), and XGBoost (sequential bias correction through boosted trees). Each algorithm captures the exposure-outcome relationship differently, and ensemble predictions that weight across algorithms consistently outperform any single model in high-dimensional, correlated-predictor settings like those we face here<sup>27,28</sup> . This combination (elastic net, random forest, and XGBoost) has been used to generate exposome risk scores in multi-cohort epidemiological studies<sup>52</sup> , and XGBoost in particular has demonstrated strong and stable performance predicting frailty and functional aging outcomes from EHR-derived and environmental data in older adult populations<sup>53</sup> . Final ensemble predictions are a weighted average of the three algorithms, with weights proportional to cross-validated performance. Each algorithm is tuned via 10-fold cross-validation designed to respect temporal order, preventing data leakage across waves. We evaluate final model performance on a held-out 20% test set, reporting RMSE per outcome and calibration plots stratified by exposure quartile. For each outcome, we will additionally report threshold-specific calibration statistics at clinical cutoff to confirm that the ensemble performs reliably at the regions of the outcome distribution most relevant to clinical and policy interpretation. 

To confirm that urban exposome features contribute predictive signal beyond demographic covariates, we compute feature importance for each algorithm: SHAP values for XGBoost, permutation-based importance for random forest, and regularized coefficient magnitudes for elastic net. Features with consistently negligible importance across all three algorithms and both cohorts will be candidates for removal in a parsimonious alternative model. 

### **B.5.2. Aim 3b: Construct grounded intervention scenarios** 

We define intervention scenarios based on findings from Aims 1 and 2 and using transparent procedures<sup>54</sup> , ensuring scenario targets are grounded in the evidence this proposal generates rather than selected post hoc. An exposure qualifies for scenario modeling if it meets at least one pre-specified criterion: association with at least one aging outcome at FDR q < 0.05 in the ExWAS (Aim 1); above-median importance in the qGcomp mixture or BVCKMR (Aims 1 and 2); or involvement in a statistically significant pairwise interaction in BVCKMR (Aim 2). We will select up to five exposure targets, balancing breadth across domains with analytical tractability; where more than five qualify, final selection will be guided by association magnitude, consistency across analysis and intervention plausibility. 

Each scenario modifies exposures through a uniform threshold shift that brings all participants up to a minimum standard. For example, access to at least one greenspace within a ten-minute walk, a target already met by many neighborhoods in both cities and achievable through park development or programming in others. For each target, we define a moderate scenario (shifting exposures to the median of the top quartile of observed values) and an ambitious scenario (shifting to the 90th percentile of the observed distribution). This approach operationalizes the concept of feasible minimum risk  (scenario targets grounded in exposure levels demonstrably achieved in real populations) as distinct from theoretical optima that no existing neighborhood has reached<sup>55</sup> . 

We will additionally construct multi-component scenarios by combining two or more single-domain scenarios, for example pairing a greenspace accessibility scenario with a food environment improvement scenario. These combined scenarios are of particular practical interest to planners, because urban interventions rarely operate in isolation and the joint effect of co-occurring improvements may differ from the sum of their individual contributions. 

### **B.5.3. Aim 3c: Estimate healthy aging outcomes under intervention scenarios** 

We first establish a business as usual baseline **:** the trained ensemble is applied to each participant's observed exposure profile at the last available time point to generate predicted outcomes at _t+1_ under current environmental conditions. For each scenario, we construct a synthetic population by replacing each participant's observed exposure values with the scenario-modified values while holding all other inputs fixed, including age, sex, race and ethnicity, prior outcome values, and area-level covariates. The ensemble is applied to each synthetic population, and the difference between each scenario projection and the business-as-usual baseline, aggregated across the population, constitutes the projected population health shift under that scenario. Continuous outcomes are reported as population means under each scenario alongside the business-as-usual mean. To aid interpretation for clinical and policy audiences, we also apply established clinical thresholds to 

Page 115 

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 117 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

generate binary projections for each outcome. For VA-FI, we use the validated cutoff of > 0.21 to classify frailty<sup>43</sup> . For MMSE, we use the cutoff of ≤ 23 to indicate substantial cognitive impairment<sup>56</sup> . For PROMIS Physical Function, we use a T-score of ≤ 40 to define clinically meaningful low physical function<sup>57</sup> . For PROMIS Depression, we use a T-score of ≥ 60 as the threshold for clinically significant depressive symptoms<sup>58</sup> . For the Lubben Social Network Scale, we use the cutoff of < 12 to identify risk for social isolation<sup>59</sup> . Uncertainty is quantified through bootstrap resampling, a standard approach for propagating variability from model training through to final projections<sup>60</sup> . Results are reported as the difference between each scenario and business-asusual, with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals, per scenario and per outcome. 

### **B.5.4. Limitations and alternative approaches:** 

**(1)** Prediction models trained on observational data learn associations, not causal structures; if unmeasured confounders drive the associations the models learn, scenario projections will reflect those associations rather than the true effect of the exposure. We address this by selecting scenario targets exclusively from exposures with consistent association evidence across Aims 1 and 2, and by presenting all projections as associationbased predictions under a specified input shift, not as causal effect estimates. **(2)** If findings from Aims 1 and 2 are inconclusive or yield fewer than the planned number of qualifying exposures, we will inform scenario selection from the existing literature on urban exposures and aging-related outcomes, ensuring that Aim 3 remains executable and independent. **(3)** All scenario modifications are constrained to remain within the observed exposure distribution, because projecting outcomes under conditions outside the training data requires extrapolation the models cannot support. This constraint is also a strength: it ensures every projection reflects what urban environments have demonstrably achieved rather than theoretical optima with no empirical basis, distinguishing our approach from prior simulation studies that apply uniform percentage shifts with no grounding in observed conditions. **(4)** Holding non-exposure inputs fixed when constructing synthetic populations assumes that environmental changes do not affect neighborhood socioeconomic composition over time; in practice, urban improvements may induce gentrification that our static design cannot capture. Our projections should therefore be interpreted as short-to-medium-term estimates of the direct exposure-outcome pathway, not as long-run predictions that incorporate downstream social and economic feedback. 

### **B.6. Power Calculations:** 

**Aim 1 (MSHS EHR).** Using G*Power with Bonferroni correction across ~40 exposures (α = 0.00125), we achieve 80% power to detect effect sizes as small as 0.02 SD with our sample of ~20,000 individuals and ~160,000 person-years. For qGcomp, where a single joint effect is estimated, we apply α = 0.05, and the minimum detectable effect is smaller still. Both thresholds are well below the range of effect estimates in our preliminary data. **Aim 2 (LitCog):** Because no closed-form power formula exists for BVCKMR, we estimated the minimum detectable effect using Monte Carlo simulation (1,000 replications) with a linear mixed-effects model as a tractable analogue, targeting 80% power at α = 0.05 per outcome. In the full baseline sample (N = 900), we can detect effects as small as d = 0.04; in the reduced analytic sample at T4 (N ≈ 400), the minimum detectable effect is d = 0.07. Both thresholds fall well below the effect sizes observed in our preliminary LitCog analyses (for example, the association of PM2.5 with MMSE (β = −0.23; 95% CI: −0.34, −0.13) and NDVI with depression (β = −5.05; 95% CI: −9.51, −0.59) ) supporting adequate power across all four outcome domains, including at the latest follow-up wave when sample size is smallest. **Aim 3 (both cohorts).** Aim 3 trains prediction models rather than testing hypotheses, so adequacy is assessed in terms of model stability rather than Type I error. The MSHS dataset (~160,000 person-years) and LitCog's repeated-measures structure (~3,600 observation-waves) both substantially exceed published benchmarks for stable training of the ensemble approaches we propose. 

### **B.7. Generalizability:** 

This proposal is conducted in New York City and Chicago: two large, cosmopolitan cities with heterogeneous urban form, population composition, and neighborhood conditions. Direct city-level generalization to other US cities is not the primary claim; New York City and Chicago are not intended as representative of all urban settings. Rather, generalizability operates at the neighborhood level: both cities contain a broad spectrum of urban neighborhood types. From densely built, high-pollution, low-greenspace environments to walkable, green, mixed-use neighborhoods and the combinations of exposures present across these neighborhoods reflect conditions found throughout cities across the country. Because our analyses are operationalized at the censustract level, findings describe relationships between neighborhood-scale exposome profiles and healthy aging that are directly comparable to analogous neighborhoods in other US cities, regardless of the city in which they are located. Both cohorts recruit adults aged 55 and older from healthcare settings in large, diverse US cities, with nearly identical mean ages at baseline (63.3 vs. 63.1 years), comparable racial and socioeconomic diversity, and identical census-tract residential exposure linkage: supporting direct comparability across cohorts.

Research Strategy 



<!-- page 118 -->

Contact PD/PI: Knobel, Pablo 

### **B.8. Promoting open science and reproducibility:** 

We will adhere to several protocols to ensure rigor and reproducibility throughout this project: **(1)** prespecifying analytic plans and conducting all analyses using reproducible tools; **(2)** implementing robust data management protocols, including automated data quality checks, regular data backups with multiple secure and encrypted copies, storage in accessible non-proprietary formats, creation of metadata, and maintenance of an organized file structure; **(3)** promoting reproducible code by consistently using coding idioms and conventions, avoiding assumptions about the working environment, maintaining a predictable code structure, and providing comprehensive code annotations; **(4)** following STROBE<sup>65</sup> reporting guidelines and EQUATOR Network recommendations ; and **(5)** making all de-identified exposure layers, methods, and code publicly available under FAIR principles, consistent with NIH's Data Management and Sharing Policy — data will be deposited in NIHapproved repositories and linked with Mount Sinai's Greener geospatial platform, and all code and analytical pipelines will be maintained in open-source, version-controlled repositories, including through the lab's GitHub. 

### **B.9. Sex as a biological variable:** 

We will include both sexes in our analyses and assess the confounding, as well as potential modifying effect of sex through stratified and interaction analyses. 

### **C. Expertise and resources** 

### **C.1. Investigator expertise:** 

The research team brings precisely the complementary expertise this proposal requires, spanning exposure science, environmental epidemiology, biostatistics, machine learning, clinical geriatrics, and aging cohort science. **Dr. Knobel (PI)** is a geospatial epidemiologist whose research program centers on the urban exposome and aging-related outcomes. He has led EHR-based analyses linking urban environmental conditions to aging and cardiovascular risk in MSHS, and directs the pilot projects that generated this proposal's preliminary data. **Dr. Yitshak-Sade (Co-I)** is an environmental epidemiologist with deep expertise in mixture-based analyses of environmental health effects in older adults. She has led large-scale cohort studies using Medicare, the WTC Health Program, and MSHS. **Dr. Kloog (Co-I)** is an exposure scientist and GIS specialist recognized internationally for developing high-resolution spatiotemporal exposure models. He leads the geospatial components across all three pilot projects supporting this proposal and brings the exposure harmonization infrastructure the project requires. **Dr. Midya (Co-I)** is a biostatistician with expertise in mixture methods and interpretable machine learning for environmental health. He has supported Dr. Knobel's research program for five years. **Dr. Federman (Co-I)** is a primary care physician, Professor of Medicine, and Program Director of the NIA Aging and Dementia T32, with extensive expertise in cognitive aging, dementia screening, and clinical outcomes in older adults. **Dr. Wolf (Co-I)** is a cognitive and behavioral scientist, Director of the Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA) at Northwestern University's Feinberg School of Medicine, and PI of LitCog (R01AG030611). He provides direct oversight of LitCog data access and integrity for this proposal. **Dr. Rogers (Co-I)** is a biostatistician and co-lead of CAHRA's Measurement and Analysis Program, with expertise in longitudinal aging cohort analysis, trajectory modeling, and biological age. She is the lead analyst on LitCog and will oversee data analyst for Aim 2. 

This proposal unites two research teams with deep, first-hand knowledge of the cohorts at its core. Drs. Knobel and Yitshak-Sade have built the analytic and data infrastructure for MSHS EHR-based environmental research over the past five years. Drs. Wolf and Rogers lead LitCog and provide direct access to its data and institutional infrastructure at Northwestern. Dr. Federman has served as clinical expert for both teams across multiple projects. The two teams have been engaged in joint planning throughout proposal development and have already collaborated on the preliminary analyses reported above. 

### **C.2. Institutional Resources and Environment.** 

This proposal is based in the Department of Environmental Medicine at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, a nationally recognized center of excellence in environmental epidemiology and exposure science. The Department houses the Institute for Exposomic Research and its NIEHS-funded Center on Health and Environment Across the LifeSpan (HEALS), whose transdisciplinary exposome infrastructure directly supports this project. Geospatial and computational demands are met through high-performance computing resources and Mount Sinai Greener, the institution's open-source geospatial and environmental health repository. EHR data access is provided through the Mount Sinai Data Warehouse, built on the OMOP Common Data Model. For Aim 2, Northwestern University's Center for Applied Health Research on Aging (CAHRA) provides LitCog data infrastructure, longitudinal data management, and biostatistical support through Drs. Wolf and Rogers.

### **INCLUSION OF INDIVIDUALS ACROSS THE LIFESPAN** 

This proposal examines how urban environmental exposures shape healthy aging across its biological, physical, cognitive, and psychosocial dimensions. While healthy aging become more prevalent with advancing age, their trajectories begin well before later life. We therefore include participants aged 55 and above in both cohorts, enabling us to capture early shifts in these outcomes and trace how they evolve over time. 

The research team brings deep expertise in aging epidemiology. Dr. Knobel's research focuses on urban environmental exposures and aging-related outcomes. Dr. Yitshak-Sade has led studies linking environmental exposures to health outcomes in older adult populations. Dr. Wolf's research focuses on cognitive aging and health literacy across diverse older adult populations, with particular attention to heterogeneity in cognitive decline. Dr. Rogers brings statistical expertise in aging cohort research, with experience analyzing longitudinal data in older adult populations.

### **STUDY TIMELINE** 

This proposal involves secondary analysis of two existing cohorts (MSHS EHR and LitCog); no new data collection is required. The timeline below covers the full five-year project period. Activities overlap across years as planned, and manuscript preparation begins as soon as analytical results are available rather than waiting until the end of the project. 

### **Year 1: Infrastructure, IRB, and Exposure Database** 

Quarters 1–2. Finalize IRB approvals and data use agreements for both cohorts. Establish secure data infrastructure and access protocols. Extract MSHS EHR cohort: pull laboratory values required for PhenoAge acceleration, diagnostic and procedure codes for the VA Frailty Index, and residential address histories. Confirm LitCog data transfer under the data use agreement. 

Quarters 2–4. Build the geospatial exposure database: assemble repository datasets (NDVI, walkability, food environment, SVI, PM2.5 components, temperature, noise, ALAN, land cover), geoprocess team-developed metrics (green view index, park quality, biodiversity, crime), and extend any city-specific exposures to the second city where needed. Geocode residential addresses for both cohorts and harmonize all exposures to census tract and calendar year. Derive PhenoAge acceleration and VA Frailty Index from MSHS EHR data. Conduct data quality checks across both cohorts. Begin preliminary descriptive analyses. 

### **Year 2: Aim 1 – MSHS EHR Analyses** 

Quarters 1–2. Run ExWAS across all exposure–outcome pairs for PhenoAge acceleration and VA Frailty Index, applying Bonferroni correction. Review results and flag exposures with independent signals. 

Quarters 2–4. Run quantile G-computation (qGcomp) to estimate joint mixture effects and the relative contribution of each exposure. Conduct sensitivity analyses (alternative confounder sets, exposure lag structures, complete-case vs. imputed samples). Begin manuscript preparation for Aim 1. 

### **Year 3: Aim 2 – LitCog Analyses and Aim 1 Dissemination** 

Quarters 1–2. Fit Bayesian Varying Coefficient Kernel Machine Regression (BVCKMR) models for each of the four LitCog outcomes (physical function, cognition, depression, social wellbeing), simultaneously estimating associations with baseline levels and rates of change. Allow sufficient computation time for Bayesian model convergence. 

Quarters 2–4. Conduct sensitivity analyses for Aim 2 (alternative confounder sets, fallback linear mixed-effects qGcomp models, domain-specific BVCKMR if full mixture proves unstable). Finalize Aim 1 manuscript and submit. Begin manuscript preparation for Aim 2. Identify exposures qualifying for Aim 3 scenario modeling based on pre-specified criteria from Aims 1 and 2. 

### **Year 4: Aim 3 – Scenario-Based Projection Framework** 

Quarters 1–2. Train ensemble prediction models (elastic net, random forest, XGBoost) separately for each cohort, using 10-fold temporally ordered cross-validation. Evaluate final model performance on held-out 20% test sets. Compute SHAP and feature importance metrics. 

Quarters 2–4. Define grounded intervention scenarios (up to five exposure targets, moderate and ambitious thresholds, single-domain and multi-component scenarios). Construct synthetic populations and apply trained models to generate scenario projections. Quantify uncertainty via bootstrap resampling. Finalize Aim 2 manuscript and submit. Begin manuscript preparation for Aim 3. 

### **Year 5: Dissemination, Integration, and Close-Out** 

Quarters 1–2. Finalize Aim 3 manuscript and submit. Prepare an integrated synthesis paper drawing on findings across both cohorts and all aims. Present findings at national conferences (e.g., ISEE, AHA, GSA). 

Quarters 2–4. Deposit all de-identified geospatial exposure layers and analytical code in NIH-approved repositories under FAIR principles, linked with the Mount Sinai GREENER platform. Complete project close-out activities including final progress report. Explore opportunities for follow-on funding based on findings.

### **PROTECTION OF HUMAN SUBJECTS** 

### **A. Human Subjects Involvement, Characteristics, and Design** 

This proposal involves secondary analysis of data from two existing cohorts. No new participants will be recruited, and neither cohort requires participant contact for the purposes of this study. 

**Cohort 1: Mount Sinai Health System Electronic Health Records (MSHS EHR).** MSHS serves approximately 3 million people annually across the five boroughs of New York City, Westchester, and Long Island. We will analyze data on approximately 20,000 patients aged 55 and older with up to 14 years of follow-up. The dataset includes sociodemographic and clinical information such as address, age, race, sex, mortality date, laboratory values, procedures, measurements, and ICD-coded conditions. We will assign environmental exposures to participants by residential address and measurement dates. 

**Cohort 2: Health Literacy and Cognitive Function Among Older Adults (LitCog).** LitCog is a longitudinal cohort study of approximately 900 community-dwelling adults aged 55–74 at baseline, recruited from primary care practices throughout Chicago, Illinois. The cohort has been followed across multiple waves over 15 years. The study is approved by Northwestern University’s Institutional Review Board, and informed consent was obtained from all participants at enrollment and re-obtained at each subsequent wave. We will use existing data shared under a data use agreement; no new participant contact is required for this proposal. 

### **B. Sources of Materials** 

For the MSHS EHR cohort, all data will be obtained through the Mount Sinai Data Warehouse (MSDW). MSDW collects and organizes clinical and operational data derived from patient care processes across the Mount Sinai Health System, sourced from Epic and ancillary systems. It contains over 4 billion facts across 9.3 million patient records and is updated daily. It is compliant with New York State and HIPAA regulations and with Mount Sinai IRB policies. No participants will be contacted, interviewed, or examined. For the LitCog cohort, data were collected through in-person and telephone interviews assessing cognitive function, physical function, depression, social wellbeing, and health literacy. Data will be shared with this project team under a formal data use agreement. No new data collection will occur. 

### **C. Potential Risks** 

The risks of participation in the proposed study are minimal, since only already collected data will be used. The only risk of our study to participants is the disclosure risk given that we require use of identifiers (addresses and dates). This will be mitigated by the data security precautions discussed in this protocol. 

### **Adequacy of Protection against Risks** 

### **A. Recruitment and Informed Consent** 

All data was collected for research purposes only. No informed consent is necessary since the data had been already collected as part of routine health encounters. 

### **B. Protection against Risk** 

All data used within this during this study will be kept confidential in accordance with Institutional research policies and HIPAA law. The Investigator and other site personnel will not use such data and records for any purpose other than conducting the study. We will comply with all Mount Sinai’s security guidelines. The data will be stored on an encrypted computer behind the firewall and locked in an office at Mount Sinai. Access will be limited to personnel listed in the approved PPHS (program for protection of human subjects) protocol. Data will not be stored on removable media. All geocoding will be done offline. No sharing will occur with anyone, in any form, without prior approval by the PPHS. Data will be stored for the duration of the study and destroyed at the time of expiration of the study. 

### **Potential Benefits of the Proposed Research to the Subjects and Others** 

There are no direct benefits to the individual study participants.

### **Importance of Knowledge to be Gained** 

There is great potential benefit to society in terms of knowledge gained regarding environmental exposures and healthy aging. Because the subjects in the proposed study are not at any obvious future risk, the risk/benefit ratio is minimal. We will conduct this research in the belief that societal benefits, in the form of knowledge and its potential application, will be tangible.

### **DATA AND SAFETY MONITORING PLAN** 

This study leveraging two retrospective cohorts will involve no participant contact. The only risk of our study to participants is the disclosure risk given that we require the use of geographic identifiers. This will be mitigated by the data security precautions discussed below. All study personnel have completed the ISMMS required training in HIPAA and the protection of human subjects coursework via the CITI training. We will comply with all ISMMS security guidelines. The data will be stored on an encrypted computer behind the firewall and locked in an office at Mount Sinai. Access will be limited to personnel listed in the approved PPHS protocol. Data will not be stored on removable media. All geocoding of addresses will be done offline. No sharing will occur with anyone, in any form, without prior approval by the PPHS. Data will be destroyed at the time of expiration of this study and documented appropriately.
